Let me refine this design: ```cuda template<int Br=64, int Bc=128, int D=128, int NUM_THREADS=256> __global__ void gqa_attention_kernel(half* out, const half* q, const half* k, const half* v, int batch, int num_q_heads, int num_kv_heads, int seq_len, float scale) { // Grid: (seq_tile, kv_head, batch) int seq_tile = blockIdx.x; int kv_head = blockIdx.y; int b = blockIdx.z; int q_heads_per_kv = num_q_heads / num_kv_heads; int q_head_start = kv_head * q_heads_per_kv; int tid = threadIdx.x; int warp_id = tid / 32; int lane_id = tid % 32; // Shared memory extern __shared__ char smem[]; half* k_smem = (half*)smem; // Bc x D half* v_smem = (half*)&k_smem[Bc * D]; // Bc x D float* s_smem = (float*)&v_smem[Bc * D]; // Br x Bc half* q_smem = (half*)&s_smem[Br * Bc]; // Br x D int q_start = seq_tile * Br; int q_end = min(q_start + Br, seq_len); // Process each query head that shares this KV head for (int qh = 0; qh < q_heads_per_kv; qh++) { int q_head = q_head_start + qh; // Load Q tile into shared memory const half* q_ptr = q + ((b * num_q_heads + q_head) * seq_len + q_start) * D; for (int i = tid; i < Br * D; i += NUM_THREADS) { int row = i / D; int col = i % D; if (q_start + row < seq_len) { q_smem[i] = q_ptr[row * D + col]; } else { q_smem[i] = __float2half(0.0f); } } __syncthreads(); // Initialize softmax statistics float m[Br]; // max float l[Br]; // sum float o[Br][D/NUM_THREADS or similar] // output accumulation // Actually keeping O in registers per thread is better } } ``` I realize register management for O accumulation is tricky with Br=64 and D=128. Each warp (32 threads) computes attention for 1 query token: 1. Load query vector (128 halfs) into registers - each thread loads 4 halfs 2. Iterate over KV sequence in chunks of 32 or 64 keys 3. For each chunk: - Load K block (chunk_size x 128) into shared memory - Each thread computes partial QK^T dot products for its assigned keys - Keep running max and sum for softmax 4. Here's the warp-level approach more concretely: ```cuda __global__ void attention_kernel(half* out, const half* q, const half* k, const half* v, int B, int Hq, int Hkv, int N, int D, float scale) { int tid = threadIdx.x; int warp_id = tid / 32; int lane = tid % 32; int block_warps = blockDim.x / 32; int global_warp = blockIdx.x * block_warps + warp_id; int total_warps = gridDim.x * block_warps; // Each warp handles one query position // Map warp to (batch, head, pos) int total_queries = B * Hq * N; for (int w = global_warp; w < total_queries; w += total_warps) { int pos = w % N; int tmp = w / N; int head = tmp % Hq; int batch = tmp / Hq; int kv_head = head / (Hq / Hkv); // Pointers const half* q_ptr = q + ((batch * Hq + head) * N + pos) * D; const half* k_ptr = k + ((batch * Hkv + kv_head) * N) * D; const half* v_ptr = v + ((batch * Hkv + kv_head) * N) * D; half* out_ptr = out + ((batch * Hq + head) * N + pos) * D; // Load query into registers (128 / 32 = 4 per thread) half q_reg[4]; for (int i = 0; i < 4; i++) { q_reg[i] = q_ptr[lane * 4 + i]; } // Online softmax over keys float max_score = -INFINITY; float sum_exp = 0.0f; float o_reg[128 / 32 * 2]; // Actually for output accumulation, each thread accumulates 4 floats // Wait, for output of dimension 128, each thread should accumulate 4 values float acc[4] = {0}; // Number of keys per iteration const int KV_CHUNK = 64; // Iterate over KV chunks for (int kv_start = 0; kv_start <= pos; kv_start += KV_CHUNK) { int kv_end = min(kv_start + KV_CHUNK, N); int kv_len = kv_end - kv_start; // Load K chunk into shared memory __shared__ half k_smem[KV_CHUNK * 128]; // per warp? No, shared across block // Actually each warp needs its own K and V space, or we time-share } } } ``` Hmm, shared memory across warps in a block is tricky if each warp is working independently. We could either: 1. Have each warp not use shared memory at all, load K and V directly from global memory 2. Have the block cooperate to load K/V for all warps 3. Partition shared memory per warp Option 3: With 8 warps and H100's large shared memory, we could give each warp 16KB. 8 * 16KB = 128KB for K_smem 8 * 16KB = 128KB for V_smem Total = 256KB - too much for all at once, but we can time-share. Actually we can use 64 * 128 * 2 = 16KB for K, and reuse for V. So 8 warps * 16KB = 128KB - fits! Wait, but if each warp loads its own K chunk independently, global memory loads won't be coalesced across the warp. Let me think... Actually, if all 8 warps load the SAME K chunk (since they might all be working on different query positions but the same KV head), then they can share the loaded data. But the query positions might map to different KV heads... If we launch blocks such that all warps in a block work on the same (batch, kv_head), then they all need the same K and V. In that case: - Grid: one dimension for query positions, but query positions for the same kv_head are grouped into blocks - Or better: grid = (num_kv_head_tiles, batch, num_kv_heads) where each block handles multiple query positions for one (batch, kv_head) Let me redesign: - Each block = 8 warps - All warps in a block handle query positions from heads that map to the SAME kv_head - Grid: (batch, kv_head) - The block iterates over query positions in chunks of 8 For 4 batches, 8 kv_heads → 32 blocks total. That's way too few! 32 blocks on 132 SMs. So we need more parallelism. We could: - Launch multiple blocks per (batch, kv_head) that handle different query position chunks - Or have each warp in a block handle a different (batch, head, pos) independently If warps are independent, shared memory sharing is hard. Let's just not use shared memory for K/V and instead use direct global memory loads with good caching. H100 has 50MB L2 cache. For seq_len=1024, each KV head is 1024*128*2 = 256KB. 8 KV heads * 4 batch = 8MB of KV data. This fits in L2! Actually 8MB easily fits in L2 (50MB on H100). So once K/V is loaded once, it stays in L2 and subsequent reads are fast. The main cost is the first read. In this case, each warp can just stream through K and V directly from global memory, and the L2 cache will serve repeated accesses. This simplifies the kernel massively! But wait, different blocks accessing the same KV data will still need to read from L2. If the same KV head is accessed by 4 query heads * 1024 positions = 4096 warps, and there are 132 SMs, L2 bandwidth should still be plenty. Let me try a simpler approach: independent warp-level attention without shared memory for K/V, using registers for Q and accumulation, and L2 for K/V caching. Actually, without shared memory for K/V, each warp loads K/V elements directly. With 32 threads in a warp, if they load contiguous elements, it's coalesced. But for computing dot products, the access pattern is: - All threads need to compare against the same query elements in sequence - Each thread might be responsible for different key positions If each thread handles 32 keys (1024/32 = 32 per thread), then: - Thread 0 handles keys 0-31, thread 1 handles keys 32-63, etc. - For dot product with query element 0: thread 0 loads K[0:31, 0], thread 1 loads K[32:63, 0], etc. - This is coalesced within each thread but not within the warp! Actually it is coalesced if we think about it right. If thread t loads K[t*32 + offset, d], these are consecutive memory locations (assuming K is stored as [N, D]). So yes, it's coalesced! Let me formalize the independent warp approach: ```cuda // Each warp handles one (batch, head, query_pos) // 32 threads in warp // Each thread handles N/32 = 32 keys (for seq_len=1024) // For dot product Q[pos,:] · K[key_idx,:]: // Each thread computes scores for 32 keys // We iterate over D in tiles, loading D elements for query and D elements for keys // Per thread arrays: // q_reg[D/8] - 16 half2 = 128 halfs, or just 4 half2 if we iterate // Actually we can load 4 halfs at a time using half2 or float2 ``` Wait, for D=128 and we use half2 operations: - q_reg[64] half2 elements = 128 halfs - But that's 64 registers, which is a lot Better: load query in chunks of say 8 half2 = 16 elements at a time, compute partial dot products, then move to next chunk. Actually, H100 has plenty of registers. 64 half2 = 128 registers * 16 bits = 64 32-bit registers. That's fine. We have up to 255 registers per thread. Let me design the kernel more carefully: ```cuda __global__ void attention_kernel( half* __restrict__ out, const half* __restrict__ q, const half* __restrict__ k, const half* __restrict__ v, int B, int Hq, int Hkv, int N, int D, float scale ) { constexpr int WARP_SIZE = 32; int tid = threadIdx.x; int warp_id = tid / WARP_SIZE; int lane = tid % WARP_SIZE; int warps_per_block = blockDim.x / WARP_SIZE; int total_queries = B * Hq * N; int warpid_global = blockIdx.x * warps_per_block + warp_id; int num_warps_total = gridDim.x * warps_per_block; for (int query_idx = warpid_global; query_idx < total_queries; query_idx += num_warps_total) { // Decode int qpos = query_idx % N; int tmp = query_idx / N; int head = tmp % Hq; int batch = tmp / Hq; int kv_head = head * Hkv / Hq; // integer division for GQA int q_per_kv = Hq / Hkv; // Pointers const half* q_ptr = q + (((int64_t)batch * Hq + head) * N + qpos) * D; const half* k_ptr = k + (((int64_t)batch * Hkv + kv_head) * N) * D; const half* v_ptr = v + (((int64_t)batch * Hkv + kv_head) * N) * D; half* o_ptr = out + (((int64_t)batch * Hq + head) * N + qpos) * D; // Load query into registers (D elements) // Use half2 for vectorized loads half2 q2[D / 2]; // 64 half2 = 128 halfs const half2* q_ptr2 = (const half2*)q_ptr; for (int i = lane; i < D / 2; i += WARP_SIZE) { q2[i] = q_ptr2[i]; } // Each thread handles N / WARP_SIZE keys // For N=1024, each thread handles 32 keys int keys_per_thread = (N + WARP_SIZE - 1) / WARP_SIZE; int kstart = lane * keys_per_thread; int kend = min(kstart + keys_per_thread, N); // Clip to causal mask: only keys <= qpos kend = min(kend, qpos + 1); // First pass: compute scores and find max float local_max = -INFINITY; float scores[32]; // up to 32 scores per thread (for N=1024) for (int kidx = kstart; kidx < kend; kidx++) { const half2* k_ptr2 = (const half2*)(k_ptr + kidx * D); float dot = 0.0f; // Vectorized dot product using half2, accumulate in float #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D / 2; d++) { half2 k_val = k_ptr2[d]; half2 q_val = q2[d]; // Manual half2 multiply-add float2 qf = __half22float2(q_val); float2 kf = __half22float2(k_val); dot += qf.x * kf.x + qf.y * kf.y; } dot *= scale; scores[kidx - kstart] = dot; local_max = fmaxf(local_max, dot); } // Warp-level max reduction float warp_max = local_max; #pragma unroll for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset /= 2) { warp_max = fmaxf(warp_max, __shfl_xor_sync(0xffffffff, warp_max, offset)); } // Compute exp and local sum float local_sum = 0.0f; for (int kidx = kstart; kidx < kend; kidx++) { scores[kidx - kstart] = expf(scores[kidx - kstart] - warp_max); local_sum += scores[kidx - kstart]; } // Warp-level sum reduction float warp_sum = local_sum; #pragma unroll for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset /= 2) { warp_sum += __shfl_xor_sync(0xffffffff, warp_sum, offset); } // Compute normalization factor float inv_sum = 1.0f / warp_sum; // Normalize scores in place for (int kidx = kstart; kidx < kend; kidx++) { scores[kidx - kstart] *= inv_sum; } // Second pass: compute output = sum(score * V) // Each thread will accumulate D / WARP_SIZE output elements... no wait // If each thread handled a range of keys, we need to now accumulate weighted V // Actually, each thread can accumulate ALL D output elements // But that requires D registers (128 floats = 128 registers) per thread // That's too much // Alternative: each thread accumulates a subset of D elements // D / WARP_SIZE = 4 elements per thread // But thread t handled keys [t*32, (t+1)*32), so we need those weights // Approach: Use warp shuffle to distribute the work // Each thread has up to 32 weights. We need to apply each weight to V[key,:] // and sum to output. // Actually, a smarter approach: // Since we have 32 threads, we can parallelize over D. // Thread d computes output[d] by having all 32 threads broadcast their scores // and corresponding V elements. // But each thread only has scores for a subset of keys. // For the keys a thread doesn't have, it gets score=0. // Let's restructure: // Thread t handles output elements t * (D/WARP_SIZE) to (t+1) * (D/WARP_SIZE) // For N=1024, each thread handles 4 output dimensions float out_reg[D / WARP_SIZE]; for (int d = 0; d < D / WARP_SIZE; d++) out_reg[d] = 0.0f; // For each key in 0..qpos: // score = (key belongs to this thread ? scores[key-kstart] : 0) // But we need the score from whichever thread owns it // Better: iterate over all keys. For keys in this thread's range, use local score. // For keys in other threads' ranges, get score via shuffle. // Actually, we can iterate in tiles where each tile corresponds to one thread's keys // There are 32 tiles (one per thread) for (int t = 0; t < WARP_SIZE; t++) { int t_kstart = t * keys_per_thread; int t_kend = min(t_kstart + keys_per_thread, N); t_kend = min(t_kend, qpos + 1); int t_num_keys = t_kend - t_kstart; // Broadcast scores from thread t one by one for (int k = 0; k < keys_per_thread; k++) { int key_idx = t_kstart + k; float score; if (lane == t && key_idx < t_kend) { score = scores[k]; } else { score = 0.0f; } // Broadcast from thread t score = __shfl_sync(0xffffffff, score, t); if (key_idx > qpos) break; if (key_idx >= N) break; // Load V[key_idx, d] for d in this thread's output range const half* v_row = v_ptr + key_idx * D; for (int d = 0; d < D / WARP_SIZE; d++) { int dim_idx = lane * (D / WARP_SIZE) + d; half v_val = v_row[dim_idx]; out_reg[d] += score * __half2float(v_val); } } } // Write output for (int d = 0; d < D / WARP_SIZE; d++) { int dim_idx = lane * (D / WARP_SIZE) + d; o_ptr[dim_idx] = __float2half(out_reg[d]); } } } ``` Wait, this has a problem with the `__shfl_sync` inside the inner loop. For 1024 keys and 32 threads, we'd do 1024 iterations with shuffles. Each shuffle is fast, but 1024 shuffles per warp could add up. Also, there's an issue: the line `if (key_idx > qpos) break;` doesn't work correctly inside the broadcast loop because all threads need to participate in the shuffle, even if key_idx > qpos for some. Let me fix this. The issue is that all 32 threads in a warp must execute the shuffle, but they might have different qpos values. Wait, in our kernel, all threads in a warp are computing for the SAME query position! Because we mapped each warp to one (batch, head, pos). So qpos is the same for all threads in the warp. The break is fine. Actually, looking more carefully, for a given warp handling one query position: - All threads in the warp work on the same (batch, head, qpos) - The causal mask means only keys 0..qpos are valid - Each thread handles keys_per_thread = 32 keys - Thread lane handles keys [lane*32, lane*32+31] - But some threads might have kstart > qpos, meaning they handle no keys For the output accumulation: - We need to accumulate score[k] * V[k, d] for k=0..qpos and d=0..D-1 - Thread lane will compute output elements [lane*4, lane*4+3] (for D=128) Let me rewrite the accumulation more cleanly: ```cuda // Each thread accumulates D/WARP_SIZE output elements // For D=128, each thread accumulates 4 output elements // We iterate over all keys 0..qpos // For each key k, we need score[k] // score[k] is held by thread k / keys_per_thread float out_reg[4] = {0}; for (int k = 0; k <= qpos; k++) { int owner_thread = k / keys_per_thread; int local_k = k % keys_per_thread; float score = (lane == owner_thread) ? scores[local_k] : 0.0f; score = __shfl_sync(0xffffffff, score, owner_thread); // Each thread loads its portion of V[k, :] for (int d = 0; d < 4; d++) { int dim = lane * 4 + d; out_reg[d] += score * __half2float(v_ptr[k * D + dim]); } } ``` For qpos=1023, we have 1024 iterations. Each iteration: - 1 shuffle - 4 loads from V (coalesced within warp if V is row-major and threads load consecutive elements) - 4 FMAs The loads from V are to consecutive memory addresses across the warp (lane*4, lane*4+1, lane*4+2, lane*4+3 for lane=0..31). If V is stored in row-major, these are contiguous elements: V[k, 0:127]. But wait, each thread loads V[k, lane*4:lane*4+3] which are consecutive. Across the warp, we cover V[k, 0:127]. This is fully coalesced! However, this access pattern means we load V one key at a time. For the entire attention, we load V[qpos+1, 128] elements. With qpos=1023, that's 1024*128*2 = 256KB per warp. But V is in L2 cache since we also loaded K earlier. Actually, loading K and V separately means 2 passes over the KV cache. But since K and V have the same layout and we need both, we can't avoid this without storing K in shared memory during the first pass. For performance, we should process K and V in the same loop to enhance cache locality. But for the online softmax, we need all scores before computing the weighted V sum. Unless we do the online softmax trick. FlashAttention uses an online softmax trick where they keep a running sum and can incorporate new values incrementally. This allows fusing the two passes. The online softmax algorithm: - Maintain m (max), l (sum of exps), and o (output accumulator) - For each new chunk of keys: 1. Compute scores for this chunk 2. Find chunk_max 3. new_m = max(old_m, chunk_max) 4. new_l = old_l * exp(old_m - new_m) + sum(exp(scores - new_m)) 5. new_o = old_o * exp(old_m - new_m) + V_chunk^T @ exp(scores - new_m) This fuses everything into one pass! Let's use this. Here's the fused online softmax approach: ```cuda float m = -INFINITY; float l = 0.0f; float o[4] = {0}; // D/WARP_SIZE output elements for (int k_chunk = 0; k_chunk <= qpos; k_chunk += keys_per_thread) { // Wait, we need to iterate in keys_per_thread chunks because that's how scores are computed } ``` Hmm, with the warp-level approach where each thread computes scores for a chunk of keys, it's hard to do online softmax incrementally because each thread needs to compute all scores for its chunk before we can do reductions. Actually, the standard approach for warp-level online softmax is: 1. Divide keys into chunks (e.g., 32 keys per chunk or per thread's range) 2. For each chunk, compute chunk scores, find chunk max 3. Warp-reduce to find global max for this chunk 4. Update running m, l, o But if each thread handles 32 keys and we process all 32 threads' keys as one big chunk: - First, each thread computes scores for its 32 keys and finds local max - Warp-reduce to find chunk_max across all 1024 keys - Then each thread computes exp(score - chunk_max) and local sum - Warp-reduce to find chunk_sum - Then each thread can update its output accumulation Wait, but this processes ALL keys at once. For N=1024, that's fine. The issue is that for large N that doesn't fit in registers, we'd need multiple chunks. For N=1024 and each thread handling 32 keys: - Register usage: scores[32] = 32 floats per thread. That's fine (32 registers). - For even larger N, we'd need to iterate in chunks. Actually, for this specific problem N=1024, we can process all keys in one go! Let me simplify: ```cuda // Step 1: Compute all scores float max_score = -INFINITY; float scores[32]; int actual_keys = min(keys_per_thread, qpos + 1 - kstart); for (int i = 0; i < actual_keys; i++) { int kidx = kstart + i; float dot = 0.0f; const half2* k_ptr2 = (const half2*)(k_ptr + kidx * D); #pragma unroll for (int d = 0; d < D / 2; d++) { half2 k_val = k_ptr2[d]; half2 q_val = q2[d]; float2 qf = __half22float2(q_val); float2 kf = __half22float2(k_val); dot += qf.x * kf.x + qf.y * kf.y; } dot *= scale; scores[i] = dot; max_score = fmaxf(max_score, dot); } // Step 2: Warp-reduce max float warp_max = max_score; #pragma unroll for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset /= 2) { warp_max = fmaxf(warp_max, __shfl_xor_sync(0xffffffff, warp_max, offset)); } // Step 3: Compute exp and local sum float local_sum = 0.0f; for (int i = 0; i < actual_keys; i++) { scores[i] = expf(scores[i] - warp_max); local_sum += scores[i]; } // Step 4: Warp-reduce sum float warp_sum = local_sum; #pragma unroll for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset /= 2) { warp_sum += __shfl_xor_sync(0xffffffff, warp_sum, offset); } // Step 5: Normalize and accumulate output float inv_sum = 1.0f / warp_sum; for (int i = 0; i < actual_keys; i++) { scores[i] *= inv_sum; } // Step 6: Compute output float out_reg[4] = {0}; for (int i = 0; i < actual_keys; i++) { int kidx = kstart + i; const half* v_row = v_ptr + kidx * D; for (int d = 0; d < 4; d++) { int dim = lane * 4 + d; out_reg[d] += scores[i] * __half2float(v_row[dim]); } } // Step 7: Warp-level reduction of output? No! Each thread already computed its portion. // Wait, each thread computed a partial sum for its keys. But we distributed keys across threads. // So output dimension d is only partially computed by this thread (for this thread's keys). // We need to sum across all threads for each output dimension. // That's the bug! Each thread accumulates for its keys but only for its output dimensions. // We need a warp-level sum for each output dimension. for (int d = 0; d < 4; d++) { int dim = lane * 4 + d; // Thread t holds partial sum for keys [t*32, t*32+31] and dimension dim // We need to sum over t=0..31 float val = out_reg[d]; #pragma unroll for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset /= 2) { val += __shfl_xor_sync(0xffffffff, val, offset); } out_reg[d] = val; } ``` Wait, the reduction at the end is wrong. Let me think about this more carefully. Each thread lane computed: `out_reg[d] = sum_{k in lane's keys} score[k] * V[k, lane*4+d]` After this, thread lane holds a partial sum for dimension `lane*4+d` (and lane*4+d+1, lane*4+d+2, lane*4+d+3) but only for a subset of keys. To get the full sum for dimension `lane*4+d`, we need to sum the partial sums from all 32 threads. Doing a warp reduction of `out_reg[d]` using XOR shuffle will sum the values but they end up in all threads, not just the owner thread. Then thread lane would have the full sum for the wrong dimension index. Actually, a warp shuffle XOR reduction adds up values from all threads, and after the reduction, every thread has the same total sum. So after reduction, every thread has `sum_{all threads} out_reg[d]`, which is the correct total for the dimension that thread was computing. Wait, no. Let me trace through: - Thread 0: `out_reg[0] = sum over keys 0..31 of score[k] * V[k, 0]` - Thread 1: `out_reg[0] = sum over keys 32..63 of score[k] * V[k, 4]` (Ah! I see the issue - each thread is computing for a different dimension!) So we can't simply shuffle-reduce `out_reg[d]` across threads, because each thread's `out_reg[0]` corresponds to a different output dimension. We need to either: 1. Have each thread compute ALL output dimensions for its subset of keys, then no reduction needed 2. Keep it as is but do a transpose-style reduction Option 1: Each thread computes all 128 output dimensions for its 32 keys. But that requires 128 float registers per thread! That's too much. Option 2: Use a different parallelization strategy. Actually, the issue is that the parallelization over keys and the parallelization over output dimensions don't compose well with the warp shuffle. A better approach: DON'T parallelize over keys within the warp. Instead, have all 32 threads in the warp cooperate on ONE key at a time. But then how do we parallelize? We'd process keys sequentially, and all 32 threads load that key's V vector together. Then each thread multiplies by the score and accumulates its portion of the output. For a single key k: - All threads need score[k] - Thread lane loads V[k, lane*4+d] for d=0..3 - Each thread accumulates score[k] * V[k, lane*4+d] into out_reg[d] So there's no issue with keys being distributed across threads. All threads process the same key, but different dimensions. This is much cleaner! But then who computes score[k]? All threads need it. We can have one designated thread compute it, or have all threads redundantly compute it. Computing a dot product of length 128 with 32 threads cooperating: - We can partition the reduction across threads: each thread computes part of the dot product - Then warp-reduce the partial sums - This is very efficient! So the algorithm becomes: 1. Load query into registers (shared by all threads in warp via shared memory or have each thread redundantly load) 2. For each key k=0..qpos: a. All threads load a slice of K[k,:] into registers b. Each thread computes partial dot product over its slice c. Warp-reduce to get full score d. Apply scale and causal mask (if k > qpos, score = -inf) e. Apply online softmax update f. Load V[k,:] slice for each thread g. Accumulate weighted sum into output registers This is the approach used in some flash attention implementations! And it works well because: - All threads in the warp are fully synchronized naturally - Memory accesses are perfectly coalesced - Minimal register pressure Let me formalize this for H100: ```cuda // Warp-level attention for one query position // 32 threads, each handles 4 output dimensions (for D=128) __device__ __forceinline__ float warp_reduce_sum(float val) { #pragma unroll for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset /= 2) { val += __shfl_xor_sync(0xffffffff, val, offset); } return val; } __device__ __forceinline__ float warp_reduce_max(float val) { #pragma unroll for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset /= 2) { val = fmaxf(val, __shfl_xor_sync(0xffffffff, val, offset)); } return val; } // Each warp handles one (batch, head, qpos) __device__ void attention_warp( const half* q_ptr, const half* k_ptr, const half* v_ptr, half* o_ptr, int N, int D, float scale, int qpos ) { int lane = threadIdx.x % 32; int dims_per_thread = D / 32; // 4 for D=128 // Load query vector (cooperatively) // Each thread loads 4 half2 = 8 halfs? No, D/32 = 4 halfs per thread // But we use half2 loads, so each thread loads 2 half2 = 4 halfs... no that's D/64 // D=128 / 32 threads = 4 elements per thread. half2 covers 2 elements. // So each thread loads 2 half2 values. half2 q2[2]; const half2* q2_ptr = (const half2*)q_ptr; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < dims_per_thread / 2; i++) { q2[i] = q2_ptr[lane * (dims_per_thread / 2) + i]; } // Output accumulator float o_acc[4]; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < dims_per_thread; i++) o_acc[i] = 0.0f; // Online softmax state float m = -INFINITY; float l = 0.0f; // Iterate over keys for (int k = 0; k <= qpos; k++) { // Load K[k,:] slice for each thread const half2* k2_ptr = (const half2*)(k_ptr + k * D); half2 k2[2]; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < dims_per_thread / 2; i++) { k2[i] = k2_ptr[lane * (dims_per_thread / 2) + i]; } // Compute partial dot product float dot = 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < dims_per_thread / 2; i++) { float2 qf = __half22float2(q2[i]); float2 kf = __half22float2(k2[i]); dot += qf.x * kf.x + qf.y * kf.y; } // Warp reduce to full dot product dot = warp_reduce_sum(dot) * scale; // Online softmax float m_prev = m; m = fmaxf(m, dot); float exp_old = expf(m_prev - m); float exp_new = expf(dot - m); l = l * exp_old + exp_new; // Update output accumulator #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < dims_per_thread; i++) { o_acc[i] *= exp_old; } // Load V[k,:] and accumulate const half2* v2_ptr = (const half2*)(v_ptr + k * D); half2 v2[2]; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < dims_per_thread / 2; i++) { v2[i] = v2_ptr[lane * (dims_per_thread / 2) + i]; float2 vf = __half22float2(v2[i]); o_acc[i*2] += exp_new * vf.x; o_acc[i*2+1] += exp_new * vf.y; } } // Normalize output float inv_l = 1.0f / l; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < dims_per_thread; i++) { o_acc[i] *= inv_l; } // Write output half2* o2_ptr = (half2*)o_ptr; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < dims_per_thread / 2; i++) { half2 val; val.x = __float2half(o_acc[i*2]); val.y = __float2half(o_acc[i*2+1]); o2_ptr[lane * (dims_per_thread / 2) + i] = val; } } ``` This is a clean single-warp kernel for one query position. Each warp processes one query independently. For the block-level kernel: - 256 threads = 8 warps - Each block handles 8 query positions - Grid needs to cover all query positions But we can also have multiple blocks handle the same or different positions. Earlier we calculated we'd need ~16384 warps, and with 8 warps per block, that's 2048 blocks. On H100 with 132 SMs, that's ~15 blocks per SM. With good occupancy, this should be fine. But wait, 2048 blocks for 132 SMs gives about 15 blocks per SM. Each block uses maybe 64 registers per thread + some shared memory (which we don't need!). Actually this warp-level kernel doesn't use shared memory at all for K/V! It uses registers for q2 and k2/v2. Actually q2 has 2 half2 = 4 halfs per thread, and similarly for k2, v2. So register usage is low. We could fit multiple blocks per SM. But there's an optimization: we can use shared memory to cache the query vector, so all warps in the same block that belong to the same head can share it. Actually, for GQA, 4 query heads share the same KV head, but have different query vectors. Hmm, we can also load K and V into shared memory for the block if multiple warps share the same (batch, kv_head) but different query positions. They all need the same K and V. So if a block handles 8 query positions for the same (batch, kv_head), they can share K and V through L2 cache or shared memory. Actually, loading K/V into shared memory for the block is tricky because K and V are large (256KB for 1024*128 fp16). That's more than shared memory. But we can load them in chunks. Let me think... Actually for H100, L2 cache is 50MB and has very high bandwidth. For batch=4, Hkv=8, N=1024, D=128: - Total K cache size: 4 * 8 * 1024 * 128 * 2 = 8MB - Total V cache size: 8MB - Total: 16MB This easily fits in L2! So once K and V are loaded from HBM, subsequent accesses are served by L2. For our kernel, each SM will be reading K and V. Since each query position reads all keys up to its position, and there are 1024 positions per head, the total K reads per head = 1024 * 1024 * 128 * 2 = 256MB *somthing... wait. Actually each query position reads all keys 0..qpos. Average qpos is ~512. So average keys read per query = 512. Total key reads = 4 * 32 * 1024 * 512 * 128 * 2 = 17GB (for K) + 17GB (for V) = 34GB of reads. This is a lot, but with L2 caching, each key is read ~512 times on average per head, but from L2 after the first read. Wait, that's not right. For a given KV head, all query positions in that head's group read the same KV data. There are 4 query heads per KV head, each with 1024 positions, so 4096 queries reading the same KV head. The total data for one KV head is 1024 * 128 * 2 = 256KB. This is tiny and stays in L2. So our kernel is actually very L2-friendly! We just need to ensure multiple warps/threads accessing the same KV head are scheduled in a way that benefits from L2. For GQA optimization, we should definitely group query positions by KV head. That is: - A block handles 8 query positions, and these 8 positions should all use the SAME KV head - This way, all warps in the block read the same K and V data, maximizing L2 hit rate So our grid should be designed such that query positions for the same KV head are grouped in the same block or nearby blocks. Here's my block design: - Each block handles 8 query positions from potentially different query heads - BUT all 8 query heads must map to the SAME (batch, kv_head) - Since there are 4 query heads per KV head, a block can handle 2 query heads * 4 positions each, or 4 query heads * 2 positions, or 8 positions from one query head, etc. For simplicity, let's say each warp handles one (batch, q_head, qpos). And a block contains 8 warps. We want these 8 warps to all have the same (batch, kv_head). Mapping: - Query positions are numbered 0..(B*Hq*N - 1) - Each block handles 8 consecutive positions in this numbering - We need to ensure all 8 have the same batch and kv_head If we order query positions by (batch, kv_head, q_head, pos), then consecutive positions might have different q_head but same kv_head. That's fine! Actually if we order by (batch, kv_head, pos, q_head), then positions for the same KV head and same sequence position but different query heads are consecutive. With Hq/Hkv = 4 query heads per KV head, we'd have 4 consecutive positions with the same batch, kv_head, and qpos. Hmm, but we want to maximize the number of query positions per block. Let me think of the total work: - 4 batches * 8 kv_heads = 32 KV heads total - Each KV head serves 4 query heads * 1024 positions = 4096 query positions - Total positions = 131072 If each block handles 8 query positions all for the same KV head: - That's 4096 / 8 = 512 blocks per KV head... no wait. - Each KV head has 4 Q heads * 1024 positions = 4096 query positions - Each block handles 8 positions → 4096 / 8 = 512 blocks per KV head - Total blocks = 32 * 512 = 16384 blocks - This is perfect! 16384 blocks on 132 SMs = ~124 blocks per SM - And each block's 8 warps all access the same K and V data → excellent L2 reuse! But we also need to specify WHICH 8 query positions go in each block. We can tile by (pos, q_head): - Block 0: batch=0, kv_head=0, positions (0,0), (0,1), (0,2), (0,3) for q_head=0, and (1,0), (1,1), (1,2), (1,3) for q_head=1 - Actually this gets complicated. A simpler mapping: iterate over all query positions in a specific order. Global query pos index = ((batch * Hq + q_head) * N + pos) If we want all warps in a block to share the same kv_head, we need: - warp0: (b0, h0, p0) where h0 maps to kv_head k - warp1: (b0, h1, p1) where h1 maps to kv_head k - etc. For a given (batch, kv_head), there are q_per_kv = Hq/Hkv = 4 query heads, each with N=1024 positions = 4096 query positions. If each block handles 8 query positions for this (batch, kv_head), and we have 4096 positions, we need 512 blocks per (batch, kv_head). The simplest mapping: flat index over query positions for this (batch, kv_head). For (batch, kv_head), we have query heads q_head_start .. q_head_start + 3. Each query head has 1024 positions. Total = 4096 positions. Block handles positions [blockIdx * 8, blockIdx * 8 + 7). For position idx in 0..4095: - q_head_offset = idx / 1024 = 0, 1, 2, or 3 - pos = idx % 1024 - q_head = q_head_start + q_head_offset Here's the kernel: ```cuda __global__ void gqa_attention_kernel( half* __restrict__ out, const half* __restrict__ q, const half* __restrict__ k, const half* __restrict__ v, int B, int Hq, int Hkv, int N, int D, float scale ) { constexpr int WARP_SIZE = 32; constexpr int WARPS_PER_BLOCK = 8; // 256 threads / 32 int tid = threadIdx.x; int warp_id = tid / WARP_SIZE; int lane = tid % WARP_SIZE; // Global block mapping: // grid has enough blocks to cover all (batch, kv_head) position groups // Each block handles WARPS_PER_BLOCK query positions int total_kv_heads = B * Hkv; int positions_per_kv_head = (Hq / Hkv) * N; // 4096 int global_block = blockIdx.x; int kv_head_idx = global_block / (positions_per_kv_head / WARPS_PER_BLOCK); int local_block = global_block % (positions_per_kv_head / WARPS_PER_BLOCK); if (kv_head_idx >= total_kv_heads) return; int batch = kv_head_idx / Hkv; int kv_head = kv_head_idx % Hkv; int q_head_start = kv_head * (Hq / Hkv); // Each warp handles one query position int local_warp_pos = local_block * WARPS_PER_BLOCK + warp_id; if (local_warp_pos >= positions_per_kv_head) return; int q_head_offset = local_warp_pos / N; int qpos = local_warp_pos % N; int q_head = q_head_start + q_head_offset; // Pointers const half* q_ptr = q + (((int64_t)batch * Hq + q_head) * N + qpos) * D; const half* k_ptr = k + (((int64_t)batch * Hkv + kv_head) * N) * D; const half* v_ptr = v + (((int64_t)batch * Hkv + kv_head) * N) * D; half* o_ptr = out + (((int64_t)batch * Hq + q_head) * N + qpos) * D; // Load query (cooperatively within warp, but each thread loads its slice) int dims_per_thread = D / WARP_SIZE; // 4 for D=128 half2 q2[dims_per_thread / 2]; const half2* q2_ptr = (const half2*)q_ptr; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < dims_per_thread / 2; i++) { q2[i] = q2_ptr[lane * (dims_per_thread / 2) + i]; } // Output accumulator (in registers, fp32 for numerical stability) float o_acc[dims_per_thread]; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < dims_per_thread; i++) o_acc[i] = 0.0f; // Online softmax state float m = -INFINITY; float l = 0.0f; // Iterate over keys // All warps in this block iterate over the SAME keys (same kv_head) // This provides excellent L2 cache locality for (int k = 0; k <= qpos; k++) { // Each thread loads its portion of K[k,:] const half2* k2_ptr = (const half2*)(k_ptr + k * D); half2 k2[dims_per_thread / 2]; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < dims_per_thread / 2; i++) { k2[i] = k2_ptr[lane * (dims_per_thread / 2) + i]; } // Compute partial dot product float dot = 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < dims_per_thread / 2; i++) { float2 qf = __half22float2(q2[i]); float2 kf = __half22float2(k2[i]); dot += qf.x * kf.x + qf.y * kf.y; } // Warp reduce to get full dot product dot = warp_reduce_sum(dot) * scale; // Online softmax float m_prev = m; m = fmaxf(m, dot); float exp_old = expf(m_prev - m); float exp_new = expf(dot - m); l = l * exp_old + exp_new; // Update output #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < dims_per_thread; i++) { o_acc[i] *= exp_old; } // Load V[k,:] and accumulate const half2* v2_ptr = (const half2*)(v_ptr + k * D); half2 v2[dims_per_thread / 2]; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < dims_per_thread / 2; i++) { v2[i] = v2_ptr[lane * (dims_per_thread / 2) + i]; float2 vf = __half22float2(v2[i]); o_acc[i * 2] += exp_new * vf.x; o_acc[i * 2 + 1] += exp_new * vf.y; } } // Normalize float inv_l = 1.0f / l; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < dims_per_thread; i++) { o_acc[i] *= inv_l; } // Write output half2* o2_ptr = (half2*)o_ptr; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < dims_per_thread / 2; i++) { half2 val; val.x = __float2half(o_acc[i * 2]); val.y = __float2half(o_acc[i * 2 + 1]); o2_ptr[lane * (dims_per_thread / 2) + i] = val; } } ``` For H100 specifically, a few more optimizations: 1. Use `rcp` for 1/l approximation if accuracy allows, but for attention we probably want exact division 2. Use `__fmax_rn` for deterministic max 3. Use `__expf` which maps to fast hardware intrinsic on H100 4. Consider using 64 or 128 threads per warp group (but standard warp is 32) Wait, on Hopper there's actually the concept of warpgroups (4 warps = 128 threads) and cluster synchronization. But for simplicity and to ensure correctness, standard CUDA should be fine. One potential improvement: for H100, we can use `__half2half2` or similar packed operations more aggressively. But the current code already uses half2 for loads. Another H100 optimization: use async global to shared memory copy (`cp.async`) to prefetch K and V. But since we're reading K and V sequentially and the data fits in L2, this might not be necessary. However, prefetching could help hide latency. Actually, a bigger optimization for H100: Since L2 cache is so large and fast, and since we're memory bandwidth bound, the main thing is to ensure good memory access patterns. Our access pattern for K and V is: - For key k: all threads read K[k, 0:D] in a coalesced manner (each thread reads 4 consecutive elements, together covering 0:D) - This is perfectly coalesced within each warp - And since all warps in a block read the same keys sequentially, we get good L2 hit rate For the query: each thread loads its query slice once and reuses it across all keys. This is also efficient. For the output: each thread writes its output slice once. Coalesced. Now for the grid launch: ```cuda int positions_per_kv_head = (Hq / Hkv) * N; int total_blocks = B * Hkv * (positions_per_kv_head / WARPS_PER_BLOCK); ``` For B=4, Hkv=8, positions_per_kv_head=4096, WARPS_PER_BLOCK=8: total_blocks = 4 * 8 * 512 = 16384 This is a good number of blocks for H100. Wait, positions_per_kv_head must be divisible by WARPS_PER_BLOCK. 4096 / 8 = 512, which is exact. Good. If it's not divisible, we'd need ceiling division and handle overflow. For H100, we might also want to use larger blocks. What if we use 16 warps per block (512 threads)? - positions_per_kv_head / 16 = 256 blocks per kv_head - Total blocks = 4 * 8 * 256 = 8192 - 8192 blocks on 132 SMs = 62 blocks per SM - Still fine, and each SM has fewer concurrent blocks Actually, occupancy might be better with smaller blocks. Let me estimate register usage: - q2: 2 half2 = 4 registers (half counts as half a 32-bit register? or a full one? Each half is 16 bits, half2 is 32 bits. So 2 half2 = 2 registers) - k2: 2 registers - v2: 2 registers - o_acc: 4 floats = 4 registers - dot, m, l, etc.: ~10 registers - Total: ~25 registers per thread With 255 max, we could fit many warps. But the real limit might be the warp scheduler or block dispatch. Let me check H100 limits. H100 has: - Max 32 thread blocks per SM - Max 2048 threads per SM - So with 256 threads per block, max 8 blocks per SM - With 512 threads per block, max 4 blocks per SM - With 128 threads per block, max 16 blocks per SM Given our low register usage, 256 threads per block seems like a good sweet spot. Actually wait - with 16384 blocks, if each SM can run 8 blocks concurrently, and there are 132 SMs, max concurrent blocks = 1056. So we'd need 16384 / 1056 ≈ 16 waves. That's plenty to saturate the GPU. Now, for the actual ModelNew code, I need to wrap this in PyTorch C++ extension. One concern: numerical precision. The reference might use fp32 accumulation, and we need to match within tolerance. Our kernel uses: - fp16 for inputs/outputs - fp32 for dot product accumulation - fp32 for softmax - fp32 for output accumulation - Then cast back to fp16 This should match standard attention implementations well. Another concern: the hidden reference implementation. It might be using standard PyTorch `F.scaled_dot_product_attention` or a custom flash attention. Our implementation needs to match numerically. For GQA, we need to be careful about how query heads map to KV heads. Standard GQA: - q_head 0-3 → kv_head 0 - q_head 4-7 → kv_head 1 - etc. Our mapping: `kv_head = q_head / q_per_kv` where `q_per_kv = Hq / Hkv`. OK, let me now write the full kernel. But before I finalize, let me think about whether there are any other optimizations for H100. 1. **Async copy**: We could use `__pipeline_memcpy_async` or `cp.async` to prefetch the next key's K and V while computing the current one. This is a Hopper feature! ```cuda // Prefetch K[k+1,:] while computing dot product for K[k,:] ``` 2. **Tensor cores**: We could use HMMA for the dot products. But for D=128 and processing one key at a time, tensor cores might not be efficient due to small matrix sizes. 3. **Larger vector loads**: We could use `float4` loads instead of `half2`, loading 8 halfs at once. This reduces instruction count. Let me try using float4 for loads. Each thread loads D/32 = 4 elements. float4 contains 8 halfs. So we can't use float4 directly unless we process 2 dimensions per load and have each thread load 8 elements (D=256). Alternatively, we can restructure so each thread handles 8 dimensions (D=128 / 16 threads = 8... but we have 32 threads). Actually for D=128 and 32 threads, float4 might not divide evenly unless we adjust. Let's stick with half2 which is clean. Wait, H100 supports `__nv_bfloat162` and has very high bf16 throughput. But the inputs are fp16, so we should use fp16. One more optimization: for the online softmax, we're doing: ```cuda float m_prev = m; m = fmaxf(m, dot); float exp_old = expf(m_prev - m); float exp_new = expf(dot - m); l = l * exp_old + exp_new; ``` This is standard. But we can save the `exp_new` values to avoid recomputing later. Actually in our code, we don't recompute - we use `exp_new` directly for the V accumulation. That's correct. OK, I think my kernel design is solid. Let me write it. Wait, one more thing: shared memory for the query vector. Currently, each thread in the warp redundantly loads its portion of Q from global memory. Since all warps in a block might be working on different Q heads, there's no sharing opportunity. But for warps working on the same Q head but different positions, they need different Q vectors anyway. For warps in the same block working on the SAME Q head but DIFFERENT positions, they can't share Q either because each position has its own query vector. So global memory loads for Q are fine. But wait, for GQA, a block handles 8 query positions. These might come from: - Positions 0,1,2,3 for q_head=0 and positions 0,1,2,3 for q_head=1 - All mapping to the same kv_head In this case, warp 0-3 work on q_head=0, pos=0,1,2,3 Warp 4-7 work on q_head=1, pos=0,1,2,3 Each warp loads its own Q vector from global memory. There's some locality since positions 0,1,2,3 for the same head are adjacent in memory. Might benefit from L2/texture cache. OK, I think the kernel is good. Let me also think about whether to use shared memory at all. Given that L2 can handle the K/V caching, and our register-based approach is efficient, no shared memory is needed. This simplifies things. Actually, one optimization with shared memory: we can have the block load a chunk of K/V into shared memory and then have all warps use it. Since all warps in a block need the same K and V, this could reduce global memory bandwidth. For example, for K: - Block has 8 warps, all reading keys 0..qpos - We can load keys in chunks of, say, 64 into shared memory - Each chunk is 64 * 128 * 2 = 16KB - 8 warps * 32 threads = 256 threads to load 16KB = 64 halfs per thread - After loading, each warp reads from shared memory instead of global memory This is a tradeoff: - With shared memory: load K chunk once from global, all warps read from shared - Without shared memory: each warp loads K independently from L2 Given H100's massive L2 bandwidth and our relatively few blocks per SM, the shared memory approach might actually be slower due to the synchronization overhead. Let me just stick with the pure register/L2 approach. It's simpler and likely very competitive on H100. However, there's one more thing I should consider: the memory access pattern for K and V. In our kernel, each warp iterates over k from 0 to qpos. For key k, the warp loads K[k, :]. If qpos is small (e.g., 0), we only load 1 key. If qpos is 1023, we load 1024 keys. But different warps in the same block have different qpos values (because they handle different query positions). So: - Warp 0 (qpos=0): loads K[0, :] - Warp 1 (qpos=1): loads K[0:1, :] - Warp 2 (qpos=2): loads K[0:2, :] - etc. If positions in a block are consecutive, warps collectively load K[0], K[0], K[1], K[0], K[1], K[2], etc. There's some redundancy but it's not terrible. Actually, let me check: if a block handles positions 0,1,2,3,4,5,6,7 for some head, then: - Warp 0 loads K[0] - Warp 1 loads K[0], K[1] - Warp 2 loads K[0], K[1], K[2] - ... - Warp 7 loads K[0], K[1], K[2], K[3], K[4], K[5], K[6], K[7] So key K[0] is loaded 8 times, K[1] is loaded 7 times, etc. The first few keys have significant redundant loads. Shared memory can help here! If we load a chunk of K (say 64 keys) into shared memory, all warps can read from it without redundant global loads. Let me add shared memory caching for K and V tiles. With 256 threads and a K tile of 64 * 128 halfs = 16KB: - 16KB / 256 threads = 64B per thread - Each thread can load 32 halfs = 64B using 16 half2 loads, or 4 float4 loads - This is very efficient And we need V tile too: another 16KB Total shared memory per block: 32KB Plus maybe a tile for Q if we want to share across warps (but not needed at first) For H100 with 228KB shared memory per SM and 32 max blocks per SM, 32KB per block means 7 blocks could fit in shared memory simultaneously. With 256 threads per block, and max 2048 threads per SM, that's 8 blocks max anyway. So 32KB is a good size. Let's redesign with shared memory tiles: - Tile size: K_TILE = 64 keys → 16KB - V_TILE = 64 keys → 16KB - Total shared = 32KB Algorithm: ``` For each K/V tile of 64 keys: 1. Load K tile to shared memory (all 256 threads cooperate) 2. Load V tile to shared memory (all 256 threads cooperate) 3. __syncthreads() 4. Each warp processes query positions that need keys from this tile - Warp with qpos=p needs keys 0..p. So it processes this tile if tile_start <= p - For key k in tile where k <= qpos and k < tile_end: - Load K[k,:] from shared memory - Compute score - Online softmax update - Load V[k,:] from shared memory - Accumulate output ``` This drastically reduces redundant global memory loads! For a block handling positions 0..7: - First tile (keys 0..63): load once, all 8 warps use it - Subsequent tiles: same For seq_len=1024 and tile_size=64, we have 16 tiles. Each tile is loaded once per block, not once per warp. This reduces global K/V bandwidth by up to 8x (the number of warps per block)! This is a huge win. Let me implement this. Actually, wait. For warps with qpos=0, they only need key 0. For qpos=7, they need keys 0..7. So the first tile covers all warps. For tile j (keys j*64 to j*64+63): - Warp with qpos=p needs this tile only if j*64 <= p - But we load all tiles anyway and warps skip keys they don't need With 16 tiles total for 1024 keys: - Total shared memory loads from global: 16 tiles * 16KB = 256KB per block - Without shared memory: 8 warps * average qpos=512 keys * 128 halfs * 2 bytes = 8 * 512 * 256 = 1MB for K, same for V = 2MB - Savings: 8x for K and V! Actually wait, the warps without shared memory also iterate over all keys up to qpos. With qpos up to 1023, average is ~512. 8 warps * 512 = 4096 key reads. With shared memory, we load 16 tiles once each, so 1024 unique key reads regardless of how many warps. Actually 16 tiles * 64 keys = 1024 keys, yes. So we load each key exactly once. With 8 warps, that's 8x reduction in K/V reads from global memory! This is huge. Let me implement this properly. Wait, but there's a subtlety. The block handles positions that might not all be from the same head. If warp 0 handles q_head=0, pos=0 and warp 4 handles q_head=1, pos=0, they have the same K and V (same kv_head) but different Q. So they can share K and V. That's correct. OK, let me write the optimized kernel with shared memory: ```cuda template<int D, int WARPS_PER_BLOCK, int KV_TILE> __global__ void gqa_flash_attention_kernel(...) { int tid = threadIdx.x; int warp_id = tid / 32; int lane = tid % 32; // Block mapping int positions_per_kv_head = (Hq / Hkv) * N; int total_kv_head_blocks = (positions_per_kv_head + WARPS_PER_BLOCK - 1) / WARPS_PER_BLOCK; int global_block = blockIdx.x; int kv_head_idx = global_block / total_kv_head_blocks; int local_block = global_block % total_kv_head_blockss; if (kv_head_idx >= B * Hkv) return; int batch = kv_head_idx / Hkv; int kv_head = kv_head_idx % Hkv; int q_head_start = kv_head * (Hq / Hkv); int q_per_kv = Hq / Hkv; // Each warp's query position int local_pos = local_block * WARPS_PER_BLOCK + warp_id; // For local_pos, decode which (q_head, pos) it is // We iterate by q_head first, then pos? Or pos first, then q_head? // Let's do: for a given kv_head, positions are ordered as: // (q_head=0, pos=0), (q_head=0, pos=1), ..., (q_head=0, pos=N-1), // (q_head=1, pos=0), ... // So local_pos / N = q_head_offset, local_pos % N = pos int q_head_offset = local_pos / N; int pos = local_pos % N; int q_head = q_head_start + q_head_offset; if (q_head_offset >= q_per_kv || pos >= N) return; // Shared memory for K and V tiles // Layout: K_TILE first, then V_TILE __shared__ half smem_K[KV_TILE * D]; __shared__ half smem_V[KV_TILE * D]; // Load query into registers (each thread loads D/32 elements) int dims_per_thread = D / 32; const half* q_ptr = q + (((int64_t)batch * Hq + q_head) * N + pos) * D; half2 q2[dims_per_thread / 2]; const half2* q_ptr2 = (const half2*)q_ptr; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < dims_per_thread / 2; i++) { q2[i] = q_ptr2[lane * (dims_per_thread / 2) + i]; } // Output accumulator float o_acc[dims_per_thread]; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < dims_per_thread; i++) o_acc[i] = 0.0f; float m = -INFINITY; float l = 0.0f; // Iterate over K/V tiles const half* k_ptr = k + (((int64_t)batch * Hkv + kv_head) * N) * D; const half* v_ptr = v + (((int64_t)batch * Hkv + kv_head) * N) * D; int num_tiles = (N + KV_TILE - 1) / KV_TILE; for (int tile = 0; tile < num_tiles; tile++) { int tile_start = tile * KV_TILE; int tile_end = min(tile_start + KV_TILE, N); int tile_size = tile_end - tile_start; // All threads in block load K tile cooperatively const half* k_tile_ptr = k_ptr + tile_start * D; for (int i = tid; i < tile_size * D; i += blockDim.x) { smem_K[i] = k_tile_ptr[i]; } // Load V tile cooperatively const half* v_tile_ptr = v_ptr + tile_start * D; for (int i = tid; i < tile_size * D; i += blockDim.x) { smem_V[i] = v_tile_ptr[i]; } __syncthreads(); // Each warp processes keys from this tile that it needs // Warp for pos=p needs keys 0..p, so this tile is needed if tile_start <= p if (tile_start <= pos) { int keys_to_process = min(tile_size, pos - tile_start + 1); for (int k = 0; k < keys_to_process; k++) { // Load K[tile_start + k, :] from shared memory const half2* k2_ptr = (const half2*)(smem_K + k * D); half2 k2[dims_per_thread / 2]; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < dims_per_thread / 2; i++) { k2[i] = k2_ptr[lane * (dims_per_thread / 2) + i]; } float dot = 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < dims_per_thread / 2; i++) { float2 qf = __half22float2(q2[i]); float2 kf = __half22float2(k2[i]); dot += qf.x * kf.x + qf.y * kf.y; } dot = warp_reduce_sum(dot) * scale; // Online softmax float m_prev = m; m = fmaxf(m, dot); float exp_old = expf(m_prev - m); float exp_new = expf(dot - m); l = l * exp_old + exp_new; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < dims_per_thread; i++) { o_acc[i] *= exp_old; } // Load V and accumulate const half2* v2_ptr = (const half2*)(smem_V + k * D); half2 v2[dims_per_thread / 2]; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < dims_per_thread / 2; i++) { v2[i] = v2_ptr[lane * (dims_per_thread / 2) + i]; float2 vf = __half22float2(v2[i]); o_acc[i * 2] += exp_new * vf.x; o_acc[i * 2 + 1] += exp_new * vf.y; } } } __syncthreads(); } // Normalize and write output float inv_l = 1.0f / l; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < dims_per_thread; i++) { o_acc[i] *= inv_l; } half* o_ptr = out + (((int64_t)batch * Hq + q_head) * N + pos) * D; half2* o_ptr2 = (half2*)o_ptr; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < dims_per_thread / 2; i++) { half2 val; val.x = __float2half(o_acc[i * 2]); val.y = __float2half(o_acc[i * 2 + 1]); o_ptr2[lane * (dims_per_template / 2) + i] = val; } } ``` Wait, I need to be careful. The `__syncthreads()` is needed because all threads in the block cooperate to load shared memory, and then all warps read from it. But what about warps that don't participate in the computation? Like if `tile_start > pos` for some warps? In CUDA, `__syncthreads()` requires ALL threads in the block to reach it. So if some warps early-return or skip the sync, we get deadlock. In our code, all warps participate in the loading phase (tid loop over tile_size * D), and all warps reach __syncthreads(). Then some warps might have `tile_start > pos` and skip the computation loop, but they still need to reach the next __syncthreads() at the bottom. Since all warps reach both __syncthreads() calls (either through the computation or by falling through), we're fine. But wait! If a warp has `tile_start > pos`, it doesn't enter the `if (tile_start <= pos)` block, but it falls through to the `__syncthreads()` at the bottom. That's fine. Actually, let me double check: the warp that skips the `if` still executes `__syncthreads()`. Yes, it falls through to it. That's correct. But there's a potential issue: within the `if (tile_start <= pos)` block, we have the computation loop. If some warps enter and some don't, the __syncthreads() at the bottom is still reached by all warps. But inside the if block, there are no __syncthreads() calls. So it's fine. Actually wait, there's a subtle issue. Some warps might have `keys_to_process = 0` but `tile_start <= pos` might be true. That's fine, the loop simply doesn't iterate. OK, let me think about correctness. After the first `__syncthreads()`, all threads can safely read smem_K and smem_V. Warps that don't need this tile just skip the computation. After the computation, all threads hit `__syncthreads()` before the next tile is loaded. This is correct. Now, let me verify the I/O dimensions. In the problem: - q: [BATCH, NUM_HEADS, SEQ_LEN, HEAD_SIZE] = [4, 32, 1024, 128] - k: [BATCH, NUM_KV_HEADS, SEQ_LEN, HEAD_SIZE] = [4, 8, 1024, 128] - v: same as k - out: [BATCH, NUM_HEADS, SEQ_LEN, HEAD_SIZE] Our indexing: - `q_ptr = q + (((int64_t)batch * Hq + q_head) * N + pos) * D` This assumes q is stored in row-major as [B, Hq, N, D]. In PyTorch, tensor.stride(0) might not equal Hq * N * D if it's not contiguous. But the standard convention for get_inputs() is that tensors are contiguous. Let me assume they are contiguous or use stride arguments. Actually, the get_inputs() function creates tensors with default stride, which is row-major for dimensions [B, H, N, D]. So contiguous assumption is fine. Wait, let me double-check PyTorch's default layout. For a 4D tensor, the dimensions are (dim0, dim1, dim2, dim3) and strides are typically (s0, s1, s2, s3) where s0 = dim1*dim2*dim3, s1 = dim2*dim3, s2 = dim3, s3 = 1. Yes, that's row-major contiguous. For the ModelNew, I should pass the strides to the kernel or compute them correctly. One more optimization: instead of `__syncthreads()` for each tile, we could use async copies and double buffering. But for simplicity, let's skip this. Actually, let me consider using larger KV_TILE. With H100's 228KB shared memory, we could use KV_TILE = 128: - K: 128 * 128 * 2 = 32KB - V: 32KB - Total: 64KB For 256 threads, 64KB / 256 = 256B per thread. That's 64 halfs or 32 half2 loads per thread. Very manageable. With KV_TILE = 128: - num_tiles = 1024 / 128 = 8 tiles - Less loop overhead - Fewer __syncthreads() calls But each __syncthreads() is followed by all warps reading from shared memory. If a block handles 8 warps with widely varying qpos, many warps might need to read the entire tile, causing shared memory bank conflicts. The access pattern for K: each warp loads K[k, :] for k=0..keys_to_process. With 8 warps potentially reading different keys simultaneously, there could be bank conflicts if the keys map to the same bank. For shared memory bank conflicts: on H100, shared memory has 32 banks (same as Ampere). Each bank is 4 bytes. K is stored as half (2 bytes). If warp 0 reads K[0, lane*4:lane*4+3] and warp 1 reads K[1, lane*4:lane*4+3] simultaneously, these are addresses: - K[0, lane*4]: offset = 0*D + lane*4 = lane*4 halfs = lane*8 bytes - K[1, lane*4]: offset = 1*D + lane*4 = D + lane*8 bytes Bank for address `addr` is `(addr / 4) % 32`. - For lane 0: K[0,0] = 0 bytes → bank 0; K[1,0] = 128*2 = 256 bytes → bank (256/4)%32 = 64%32 = 0 - Bank conflict! Both warp0 and warp1 accessing bank 0. This is bad. To avoid bank conflicts, we'd need to pad K rows so that consecutive rows start in different banks. For D=128 halfs = 256 bytes, if we pad to 264 bytes (66 halfs), then row 1 starts at byte 264, bank = (264/4)%32 = 66%32 = 2. No conflict with bank 0. 264 bytes / 4 bytes per bank = 66. 66 % 32 = 2. Still no conflict for row 0 vs row 1. Actually the general formula: to avoid conflicts when multiple warps access the same column but different rows, we need row_stride_in_bytes to NOT be a multiple of 128 bytes (32 banks * 4 bytes). For D=128 halfs = 256 bytes. 256 % 128 = 0. So yes, conflict. If we pad to D_pad = 132 halfs = 264 bytes: 264 % 128 = 8. Not a multiple of 128. Good. Or simpler: we can transpose K in shared memory! Store as [D, KV_TILE] instead of [KV_TILE, D]. Then when we load K[k, :], we read a column. With 32 threads each reading a different element (at stride KV_TILE), the banks are: - K[0, k]: offset = k - K[1, k]: offset = KV_TILE + k - etc. Bank = (offset * 2 / 4) % 32 = (offset / 2) % 32 - Thread 0: (0/2)%32 = 0 - Thread 1: (KV_TILE/2)%32 If KV_TILE = 64: 32%32 = 0. Conflict! If KV_TILE = 128: 64%32 = 0. Conflict! Hmm, transposing doesn't help directly. Alternatively, we can just accept some bank conflicts. For H100, shared memory is very fast, and with low occupancy (few blocks per SM), bank conflicts might not be the bottleneck. Or, we can NOT use shared memory and rely on L2. Given H100's massive L2 bandwidth (probably >10 TB/s), and our problem fitting in L2, the register-only approach might actually be competitive or even faster than the shared memory approach with bank conflicts. Let me reconsider: without shared memory, each warp loads K and V directly from L2. With 8 warps per block and seq_len=1024, total K+V reads from L2 per block = 8 * 2 * 256KB = 4MB (for qpos averaging 512). Actually no: Each warp with qpos=p reads (p+1) keys. Average p in a block depends on how we group positions. If we group positions 0-7, average p = 3.5. Total K reads = 8 * 4 * 128 * 2 = 8KB. Total V reads = 8KB. Very small! But keys are read redundantly across warps. Key K[0] is read by all 8 warps. Key K[1] is read by 7 warps, etc. Total key accesses = sum_{k=0}^{7} (8-k) = 8+7+6+5+4+3+2+1 = 36 key vectors. 36 * 128 * 2 = 9KB of K data accessed from L2 per block. For V: same. With L2 bandwidth of ~10+ TB/s, 18KB per block is tiny. Even with 16384 blocks, total K+V accessed = 16384 * 18KB ≈ 295MB. Wait, that's for one head. 16MB total K+V for all heads. With L2, most of this is cached. Actually, the issue is that each block accesses different keys. Let me think about total memory traffic: - Total unique K data: B * Hkv * N * D * 2 = 4 * 8 * 1024 * 128 * 2 = 8MB - Total unique V data: 8MB - Total unique: 16MB With L2 caching, after the first few blocks load the data, subsequent blocks hit L2. Since all SMs are reading from the same 16MB pool, and L2 is 50MB, everything stays cached. So total HBM reads ≈ 16MB (for K and V) + Q reads + output writes. Q reads: B * Hq * N * D * 2 = 32MB Output writes: 32MB Total: ~80MB of HBM traffic. At 3.35 TB/s, this takes ~24 microseconds. Plus compute. Our kernel should be memory-bandwidth limited, and since we're minimizing HBM traffic by using L2 effectively, we should be close to optimal. Actually wait, without shared memory, are we really minimizing HBM traffic? Yes, because L2 cache holds all the unique K and V data. Once loaded by any block, it stays in L2 and serves other blocks. But with shared memory, we could ensure that each block loads K and V only once from global memory (or L2), and all warps in the block reuse it. This is even better because: - Without shared memory, each warp loads its own K and V from L2 - With shared memory, all 8 warps share one load from L2 per tile - But since L2 is so fast, the difference might be small I think for H100, given the massive L2 and the complexity of avoiding shared memory bank conflicts, the register-only warp-level approach might actually be simpler and faster. But let me hedge and provide a kernel with shared memory since it shows good optimization for the architecture. Actually, you know what, let me look at this from a competition perspective. The judges will compare against a reference PyTorch implementation. PyTorch's F.scaled_dot_product_attention uses FlashAttention or similar highly optimized kernels. To beat it, I need to be very efficient. For the specific parameters (seq_len=1024), FlashAttention-2 would be the state of the art. My shared-memory implementation is essentially a simplified FlashAttention. But with seq_len=1024 being short, a simpler kernel might actually outperform due to lower overhead. In fact, for short sequences, a "naive" attention kernel that's fully unrolled and doesn't tile over sequence can be faster than FlashAttention because there's no tiling overhead. Let me try a different block design that better matches FlashAttention-2: - Block size: 128 or 256 threads - Each block handles a tile of Br query positions for one (batch, head) - Threads cooperate to load K/V tiles of size Bc into shared memory - Compute attention using the online softmax algorithm - Parallelize O accumulation across threads For H100, with larger shared memory, we can use Br=64, Bc=256: - Q_smem: 64 * 128 * 2 = 16KB (for one head) - K_smem: 256 * 128 * 2 = 64KB - V_smem: 64KB - O accumulator in registers - Total shared: 144KB With 256 threads, this fits in shared memory. And each block handles 64 query positions for one head. - For N=1024, num_blocks_per_head = 1024 / 64 = 16 - Total blocks = 4 * 32 * 16 = 2048 blocks This gives fewer blocks than the warp-level approach (2048 vs 16384), but each block does more work and has better data reuse. For H100, 2048 blocks on 132 SMs = ~15 blocks per SM. With max 8 blocks per SM (256 threads), that's about 2 waves. This might not fully saturate the GPU. To increase parallelism, we could use smaller Br=32: - Q_smem: 32 * 128 * 2 = 8KB - K_smem: 64KB - V_smem: 64KB - Total: 136KB - Blocks per head = 1024 / 32 = 32 - Total blocks = 4 * 32 * 32 = 4096 Or we can process multiple heads per block! No. This is getting too complicated for a hand-written kernel. Let me use a simpler parallelization: each warp handles one query position, and all warps in a block share K/V through shared memory. For GQA with 4 Q heads per KV head, a block can process 2 positions from each of the 4 Q heads = 8 positions total. All share the same KV head. The warp-level shared-memory kernel I designed earlier should work well. Actually, let me look at the problem from a different angle. Given the I/O contract shows q, k, v as 4D tensors with shape [B, H, N, D], this is standard multi-head attention. The causal mask is implicit. For this problem, I think the most practical approach is a clean, correct FlashAttention-style kernel optimized for H100. Let me write one that's moderately sophisticated but not overly complex. I'll go with this design: - **Block size**: 256 threads (8 warps) - **Q tile**: Br = 64 positions - **KV tile**: Bc = 64 keys - **Parallelism**: Each block processes one query head tile (64 positions) - **Shared memory**: Q=64*128*2=16KB, K=64*128*2=16KB, V=16KB, total=48KB - **Grid**: (ceil(N/Br), Hq, B) = (16, 32, 4) = 2048 blocks For GQA: since multiple query heads share KV heads, we could launch blocks for each query head and they will naturally share KV data through L2. Wait, the I/O contract specifies `NUM_KV_HEADS = 8`, `NUM_HEADS = 32`. So indeed GQA with 4 query heads per KV head. For the block-level kernel, I'll implement a cooperative approach: ```cuda template<int Br=64, int Bc=64, int D=128> __global__ void flash_attention_gqa(...) { int tid = threadIdx.x; int warp_id = tid / 32; int lane = tid % 32; int head = blockIdx.y; int batch = blockIdx.z; int seq_tile = blockIdx.x; int kv_head = head / (Hq / Hkv); int qpos_start = seq_tile * Br; // Shared memory extern __shared__ char smem[]; half* q_smem = (half*)smem; // Br x D half* k_smem = (half*)&q_smem[Br * D]; // Bc x D half* v_smem = (half*)&k_smem[Bc * D]; // Bc x D // Load Q tile [Br, D] into shared memory // Each thread loads some elements const half* q_ptr = q + (((int64_t)batch * Hq + head) * N + qpos_start) * D; for (int i = tid; i < Br * D; i += blockDim.x) { int row = i / D; int col = i % D; if (qpos_start + row < N) { q_smem[i] = q_ptr[row * D + col]; } } // Each thread will accumulate output for some positions and dimensions // Thread mapping: each thread handles (Br / num_warps) rows // 8 warps, Br=64, so each warp handles 8 rows int rows_per_warp = Br / 8; // 8 int warp_row_start = warp_id * rows_per_warp; // Within a warp, each thread handles some columns // D=128, 32 threads, so each thread handles 4 columns int cols_per_thread = D / 32; // 4 int thread_col_start = lane * cols_per_thread; // Each thread's output accumulator: rows_per_warp * cols_per_thread floats // 8 * 4 = 32 floats per thread = 32 registers float o_acc[8][4]; // [row][col] float m[8]; // max per row float l[8]; // sum per row for (int i = 0; i < rows_per_warp; i++) { m[i] = -INFINITY; l[i] = 0.0f; for (int j = 0; j < cols_per_thread; j++) { o_acc[i][j] = 0.0f; } } // Iterate over KV tiles for (int kv_tile = 0; kv_tile < N; kv_tile += Bc) { int kv_end = min(kv_tile + Bc, N); int tile_size = kv_end - kv_tile; // Load K tile const half* k_ptr = ... + kv_tile * D; for (int i = tid; i < tile_size * D; i += blockDim.x) { k_smem[i] = k_ptr[i]; } // Load V tile const half* v_ptr = ... + kv_tile * D; for (int i = tid; i < tile_size * D; i += blockDim.x) { v_smem[i] = v_ptr[i]; } __syncthreads(); // For each of my rows for (int r = 0; r < rows_per_warp; r++) { int qrow = warp_row_start + r; if (qpos_start + qrow >= N) continue; int actual_qpos = qpos_start + qrow; // For each key in tile for (int k = 0; k < tile_size; k++) { int actual_kpos = kv_tile + k; if (actual_kpos > actual_qpos) break; // causal mask // Compute dot product Q[qrow,:] · K[k,:] float dot = 0.0f; for (int d = 0; d < cols_per_thread / 2; d++) { // Load from Q_smem and K_smem half2 q_val = ((half2*)&q_smem[qrow * D + thread_col_start])[d]; half2 k_val = ((half2*)&k_smem[k * D + thread_col_start])[d]; float2 qf = __half22float2(q_val); float2 kf = __half22float2(k_val); dot += qf.x * kf.x + qf.y * kf.y; } // Warp reduce sum over all threads (collaborative dot product) dot = warp_reduce_sum(dot) * scale; // Online softmax float m_prev = m[r]; m[r] = fmaxf(m[r], dot); float exp_old = expf(m_prev - m[r]); float exp_new = expf(dot - m[r]); l[r] = l[r] * exp_old + exp_new; // Update output accumulator for (int d = 0; d < cols_per_thread; d++) { o_acc[r][d] *= exp_old; } // Now accumulate V[k,:] weighted by exp_new for (int d = 0; d < cols_per_thread / 2; d++) { half2 v_val = ((half2*)&v_smem[k * D + thread_col_start])[d]; float2 vf = __half22float2(v_val); o_acc[r][d*2] += exp_new * vf.x; o_acc[r][d*2+1] += exp_new * vf.y; } } } __syncthreads(); } // Normalize and write output for (int r = 0; r < rows_per_warp; r++) { int qrow = warp_row_start + r; if (qpos_start + qrow >= N) continue; float inv_l = 1.0f / l[r]; for (int d = 0; d < cols_per_thread; d++) { o_acc[r][d] *= inv_l; } half* o_ptr = out + ...; for (int d = 0; d < cols_per_thread / 2; d++) { half2 val; val.x = __float2half(o_acc[r][d*2]); val.y = __float2half(o_acc[r][d*2+1]); ((half2*)&o_ptr[qrow * D + thread_col_start])[d] = val; } } } ``` Wait, this has a problem with the warp_reduce_sum inside the inner loop. The `warp_reduce_sum` does a shuffle across all 32 threads in the warp, but we're calling it inside a loop where different threads might be at different iterations. For warp_id=0, all threads in the warp handle the same set of rows (r=0..7), but they iterate over keys k. If all threads in the warp call `warp_reduce_sum` for the same key k, then the shuffle is well-defined. But different warps might be at different k values. Looking at the code: each warp handles rows `warp_row_start` to `warp_row_start + rows_per_warp - 1`. All threads in the same warp have the same `warp_id`, so they iterate over the same rows. And for a given row r, they iterate over k from 0 to tile_size. Since all threads in the warp are at the same k in lockstep (there's no divergence in the k loop), the shuffle works correctly. Wait, but `if (actual_kpos > actual_qpos) break;` causes divergence! Threads handling earlier rows (smaller qpos) will break earlier than threads handling later rows. In the same warp, threads with different r values will have different `actual_qpos`, so they might break at different k. For example, in warp 0 handling rows 0..7: - Thread for r=0 (qpos=seq_tile*Br): breaks at k > seq_tile*Br - Thread for r=7 (qpos=seq_tile*Br+7): breaks at k > seq_tile*Br+7 If seq_tile=0, r=0 has qpos=0, so breaks immediately after k=0. r=7 has qpos=7, so iterates k=0..7. This causes warp divergence! Threads in the same warp at different r values will diverge. Solution: have each warp handle only ONE row. That is, rows_per_warp = 1. Then all threads in a warp have the same qpos and same break condition. With Br=64 and 32 warps... we only have 8 warps per block. So rows_per_warp = 8 causes divergence. Alternative: rows_per_warp = 1, but then we'd need 64 warps for 64 rows, and we only have 8 warps (256 threads). So either: 1. Use rows_per_warp = 1 and process fewer rows per block (Br = 8) 2. Process all 64 rows but handle the divergence For Br=8: - Each block handles 8 query positions - Q_smem: 8 * 128 * 2 = 2KB - K_smem: 64 * 128 * 2 = 16KB - V_smem: 16KB - Total: 34KB - Blocks: (128, 32, 4) = 16384 - This is good parallelism and no divergence! But wait, 16384 blocks of 256 threads each... at 8 blocks per SM, that's 2048 concurrent blocks needed, but we only have 132 SMs * 8 = 1056. So about 16 waves. Good. With rows_per_warp = 1: each warp handles exactly 1 row. Br=8 means 8 warps, which is 256 threads. Each warp computes attention for one query position against all keys. No divergence within the warp because all 32 threads in the warp have the same qpos. But different warps in the block have different qpos. Does that matter? No, because warps in the same block but different warps can diverge without issues - they're scheduled independently. The only issue is divergence WITHIN a warp. So let's use Br=8, rows_per_warp=1, and shared memory fo K/V tiles. Wait, but then we're back to the earlier warp-level design, except now with shared memory for K/V tiles. Actually, with shared memory, the warps in a block all load the same K/V tile together, then each warp processes its row. This is very similar to my earlier design except more formalized. Key difference from earlier: Earlier I had each block handle 8 query positions that might come from DIFFERENT query heads (but same KV head via GQA mapping). Now I'm saying each block handles 8 query positions from the SAME query head. If it's the same query head, all 8 positions are for the same head, same batch. But then we'd launch 16 blocks per head (1024/8 = 128, wait 1024/8 = 128 blocks per head). 128 * 32 * 4 = 16384 blocks. Same as before. Let me just simplify. All warps share K/V tiles. Actually, I realize that if the block handles positions from the SAME query head, then the warps can't simultaneously process different positions and share K/V through shared memory efficiently because: - Warp 0 (qpos=0) only needs key 0 - Warp 1 (qpos=1) only needs keys 0,1 - ... - Warp 7 (qpos=7) needs keys 0..7 - If K/V tile is 64 keys, warps 0..7 all need keys 0..7 from the first tile, then warps with qpos < 64 skip subsequent tiles The shared memory is useful because all 8 warps read from the same loaded tile, even though they don't all read all keys. But the first few warps don't need many keys. For a block handling positions (qpos_start, qpos_start+1, ..., qpos_start+7) from the same head: - First tile (keys 0..63): all warps need it - Warp 0 needs keys 0..qpos_start - Warp 7 needs keys 0..qpos_start+7 - The shared memory is loaded once for all 8 warps If qpos_start and qpos_start+7 are both within the first tile, then we load 64 keys once and all 8 warps use them. For subsequent tiles, some warps might not need them (if qpos is small). But for the first few blocks (seq_tile=0, qpos_start=0), all warps need only the first tile. For later blocks (seq_tile=100, qpos_start=800), all warps need many tiles. So the shared memory approach provides benefit for all blocks, especially when multiple warps need the same tile. Let me implement this cleanly. Here's my final kernel design: ```cuda // Block: 256 threads (8 warps) // Each block handles 8 consecutive query positions from ONE query head // Grid: (seq_tiles, num_heads, batch) // Shared memory: K tile and V tile, each Bc * D halfs // Within a block: // - warp i handles query position (seq_tile * 8 + i) // - All warps share K/V tiles through shared memory // Thread i in warp: // - Handles output dimensions [lane*4, lane*4+3] ``` The full kernel: I realize I've been going back and forth. Let me just settle on an implementation and write it. I'll use a FlashAttention-style kernel with: - 256 threads per block - Br = 64 queries per block - Bc = 64 keys per tile - Each block handles Br queries from ONE head - Warps cooperate on loading K/V tiles - Per-warp row processing to avoid divergence Wait, for Br=64 and 8 warps, each warp handles 8 rows. Wait, but with Br=8 and block_size=256, most threads are idle during computation. Each warp handles 1 row, and there are 8 warps. For a given key k, each warp computes one score. The 32 threads in each warp compute the dot product collaboratively. So for each key k, we have 8 active warps, each computing one score. Total of 8 scores computed in parallel, using 256 threads.
