For V accumulation, same thing - 32 threads per warp accumulate for one query position. This is 100% utilization! No idle threads. OK I'll go with this. Code structure: ```cuda #define WARP_SIZE 32 #define ROWS_PER_BLOCK 8 #define KV_TILE 64 __device__ __forceinline__ float warp_reduce_sum(float val) { #pragma unroll for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset >>= 1) { val += __shfl_xor_sync(0xffffffff, val, offset); } return val; } template<int D = 128> __global__ void flash_attention_kernel( half* out, const half* q, const half* k, const half* v, int B, int Hq, int Hkv, int N, float scale ) { int tid = threadIdx.x; int warp_id = tid / WARP_SIZE; int lane = tid % WARP_SIZE; int head = blockIdx.y; int batch = blockIdx.z; int seq_tile = blockIdx.x; int kv_head = head * Hkv / Hq; int qpos_base = seq_tile * ROWS_PER_BLOCK; int qpos = qpos_base + warp_id; if (qpos >= N) return; // Shared memory for K and V tiles __shared__ half smem_K[KV_TILE * D]; __shared__ half smem_V[KV_TILE * D]; // Pointers const half* q_ptr = q + (((int64_t)batch * Hq + head) * N + qpos) * D; const half* k_ptr = k + (((int64_t)batch * Hkv + kv_head) * N) * D; const half* v_ptr = v + (((int64_t)batch * Hkv + kv_head) * N) * D; half* o_ptr = out + (((int64_t)batch * Hq + head) * N + qpos) * D; // Load query (fp16), each thread loads D/WARP_SIZE = 4 elements constexpr int DIMS_PER_THREAD = D / WARP_SIZE; // 4 half2 q2[DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2]; const half2* q2_ptr = (const half2*)q_ptr; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2; i++) { q2[i] = q2_ptr[lane * (DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2) + i]; } // Output accumulator (fp32 for numerical stability) float o_acc[DIMS_PER_THREAD]; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD; i++) o_acc[i] = 0.0f; float m = -INFINITY; float l = 0.0f; // Iterate over KV tiles int num_tiles = (N + KV_TILE - 1) / KV_TILE; for (int tile = 0; tile < num_tiles; tile++) { int tile_start = tile * KV_TILE; int tile_size = min(KV_TILE, N - tile_start); // Cooperative load of K tile const half2* k_tile_ptr2 = (const half2*)(k_ptr + tile_start * D); half2* k_smem2 = (half2*)smem_K; for (int i = tid; i < (tile_size * D) / 2; i += blockDim.x) { k_smem2[i] = k_tile_ptr2[i]; } // Cooperative load of V tile const half2* v_tile_ptr2 = (const half2*)(v_ptr + tile_start * D); half2* v_smem2 = (half2*)smem_V; for (int i = tid; i < (tile_size * D) / 2; i += blockDim.x) { v_smem2[i] = v_tile_ptr2[i]; } __syncthreads(); // Each warp processes keys from this tile int keys_needed = min(tile_size, qpos - tile_start + 1); if (keys_needed > 0) { for (int k = 0; k < keys_needed; k++) { // Compute dot product: all 32 threads in warp cooperate const half2* k2_ptr = (const half2*)(smem_K + k * D); half2 k2[DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2]; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2; i++) { k2[i] = k2_ptr[lane * (DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2) + i]; } float dot = 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2; i++) { float2 qf = __half22float2(q2[i]); float2 kf = __half22float2(k2[i]); dot += qf.x * kf.x + qf.y * kf.y; } dot = warp_reduce_sum(dot) * scale; // Online softmax float m_prev = m; m = fmaxf(m, dot); float exp_old = expf(m_prev - m); float exp_new = expf(dot - m); l = l * exp_old + exp_new; // Update output #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD; i++) { o_acc[i] *= exp_old; } // Load V and accumulate const half2* v2_ptr = (const half2*)(smem_V + k * D); half2 v2[DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2]; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2; i++) { v2[i] = v2_ptr[lane * (DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2) + i]; float2 vf = __half22float2(v2[i]); o_acc[i * 2] += exp_new * vf.x; o_acc[i * 2 + 1] += exp_new * vf.y; } } } __syncthreads(); } // Normalize float inv_l = 1.0f / l; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD; i++) { o_acc[i] *= inv_l; } // Write output half2* o2_ptr = (half2*)o_ptr; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2; i++) { half2 val; val.x = __float2half(o_acc[i * 2]); val.y = __float2half(o_acc[i * 2 + 1]); o2_ptr[lane * (DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2) + i] = val; } } ``` For the launch: ```cuda dim3 block(256); dim3 grid((N + ROWS_PER_BLOCK - 1) / ROWS_PER_BLOCK, Hq, B); flash_attention_kernel<128><<<grid, block>>>(out_ptr, q_ptr, k_ptr, v_ptr, B, Hq, Hkv, N, scale); ``` Wait, there's a subtle issue. With Bc=64 and tile_size set to min(KV_TILE, N-tile_start), for the last tile tile_size might be less than KV_TILE. But our shared memory is declared as `__shared__ half smem_K[KV_TILE * D]`, which is fixed size. And we only load `tile_size * D` elements. When reading, we only read `k` from 0 to `keys_needed - 1`, and `keys_needed <= tile_size`. So we never read uninitialized memory. Good. But wait, `keys_needed = min(tile_size, qpos - tile_start + 1)`. If tile_start > qpos, keys_needed would be negative! But we have `if (keys_needed > 0)` to guard against this. Actually `min(tile_size, qpos - tile_start + 1)` when `qpos - tile_start + 1 < 0` gives a negative number. Then the if-statement `if (keys_needed > 0)` handles it. Good. Actually, `qpos - tile_start + 1` could be negative, but computing `min` with an unsigned or wrong type could be an issue. Let me make sure it's computed with signed ints. Also, I need to handle the case where `qpos >= N` early. That's done at the top. One more issue: when multiple warps in the same block reach `__syncthreads()`, if one warp is inactive (e.g., returns early), we'd have a deadlock. But all warps are active because: - The entire block is launched for a (seq_tile, head, batch) - Each warp computes for qpos = qpos_base + warp_id - Some warps might have qpos >= N - Those warps return immediately at the top If any warp in a block returns early, the `__syncthreads()` calls will deadlock! To fix this, we need to ensure either: 1. Only launch blocks where ALL warps are active (i.e., pad N to multiple of ROWS_PER_BLOCK) 2. Or don't use early return For option 1: if N=1024 and ROWS_PER_BLOCK=8, then 1024/8 = 128 exactly. No padding needed! All warps in all blocks are active. But what if head or batch are out of range? blockIdx.y and blockIdx.z are bounded by grid dimensions, so they're always valid. Since the contract has N=1024 constant, and we can choose ROWS_PER_BLOCK to divide N, we're fine. But to be general, I should handle arbitrary N. For arbitrary N, I should avoid the early return and instead have inactive warps skip computation but still reach `__syncthreads()`. Let me restructure: ```cuda bool active = (qpos < N); // Load query if (active) { // load q2 } else { // set q2 to zero or just leave uninitialized since it won't be used } // ... in the loop ... if (active && keys_needed > 0) { // compute } __syncthreads(); ``` This way all warps reach __syncthreads(). Let me rewrite with this in mind. Also, for the output write, inactive warps shouldn't write: ```cuda if (active) { // write output } ``` OK, let me also think about whether this kernel will be faster than the reference. For N=1024, we could even process the entire attention for one query token per thread: - Each thread computes all 1024 scores and accumulates the output for its 4 output dimensions. - 32 threads per warp handle one query token. - 8 warps per block handle 8 query tokens. - Total blocks = 16384. Without shared memory, each warp loads K and V directly from global memory. Since the data fits in L2, subsequent accesses are fast. The main issue is the 1024 iterations per warp. Each iteration loads K[k,:] and V[k,:] from L2. For 32 threads, that's 4 half2 loads per thread per key. 1024 keys * 4 loads = 4096 half2 loads. At L2 latency of ~10-20 cycles, we need good latency hiding. With 8 warps per block, and multiple blocks per SM, the SM can switch between warps while one is waiting for L2 data. Actually, I think this register-only approach might be competitive with the shared-memory approach because: 1. No __syncthreads() overhead 2. No shared memory bank conflict issues 3. L2 is very fast on H100 4. Simpler code means easier for compiler to optimize Let me compare shared memory vs register-only for this kernel. With shared memory: - Load K tile once per block: 64 * 128 * 2 = 16KB from global (first time) or L2 - All 8 warps read from shared memory - Shared memory bandwidth: ~20 TB/s on H100 - No bank conflicts if accessed carefully Without shared memory (register-only): - Each warp loads K and V independently from L2 - L2 bandwidth: ~10+ TB/s (maybe higher) - 8 warps * 16KB = 128KB of K/V reads per block from L2 - L2 can handle this easily But wait - with shared memory, we load 16KB of K once from global/L2 and 8 warps reuse it. In register-only, 8 warps each load their own copy from L2, so 8 * 16KB = 128KB of L2 reads. Shared memory gives 8x reduction in L2 bandwidth for K/V! This is significant. However, there's also the advantage that in register-only, each warp loads exactly the keys it needs (up to qpos), while shared memory loads the full tile. For a warp with qpos=0, shared memory loads 64 keys but the warp only needs 1. So shared memory can waste bandwidth for early positions. For a block handling positions 0..7: - Shared memory loads tile of 64 keys: 16KB - Warp 0 needs 1 key, warp 1 needs 2 keys, ..., warp 7 needs 8 keys - Total unique keys needed = 8 - Shared memory loads 64 keys - Register-only loads: 1+2+3+4+5+6+7+8 = 36 keys - 36/64 = 56% efficiency for shared memory in this case For positions 800..807: - Shared memory loads 64 keys per tile (from tile 12 onwards) - Most warps need all 64 keys in each tile - Shared memory is much more efficient So shared memory wins for later positions, register-only is slightly better for early positions. Overall, shared memory is probably better. OK, I'll stick with shared memory. Let me finalize the kernel. Actually, one more optimization. On H100, we can use TMA (Tensor Memory Accelerator) for async data movement. But TMA requires special setup and is complex to use in a hand-written kernel. I'll skip it for now. offset > 0; offset >>= 1) { val += __shfl_xor_sync(0xffffffff, val, offset); } return val; } __global__ void gqa_attention_kernel( const half* __restrict__ q, const half* __restrict__ k, const half* __restrict__ v, half* __restrict__ out, int B, int Hq, int Hkv, int N, float scale ) { int tid = threadIdx.x; int warp_id = tid / WARP_SIZE; int lane = tid % WARP_SIZE; int head = blockIdx.y; int batch = blockIdx.z; int seq_tile = blockIdx.x; int kv_head = head * Hkv / Hq; int qpos_base = seq_tile * ROWS_PER_BLOCK; int qpos = qpos_base + warp_id; bool active = (qpos < N); __shared__ half smem_K[KV_TILE * KV_PAD]; __shared__ half smem_V[KV_TILE * KV_PAD]; const half* q_ptr = q + (((int64_t)batch * Hq + head) * N + qpos) * D; const half* k_ptr = k + (((int64_t)batch * Hkv + kv_head) * N) * D; const half* v_ptr = v + (((int64_t)batch * Hkv + kv_head) * N) * D; half* o_ptr = out + (((int64_t)batch * Hq + head) * N + qpos) * D; constexpr int DIMS_PER_THREAD = D / WARP_SIZE; half2 q2[DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2]; if (active) { const half2* q2_ptr = (const half2*)q_ptr; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2; i++) { q2[i] = q2_ptr[lane * (DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2) + i]; } } float o_acc[DIMS_PER_THREAD]; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD; i++) o_acc[i] = 0.0f; float m = -INFINITY; float l = 0.0f; int num_tiles = (N + KV_TILE - 1) / KV_TILE; for (int tile = 0; tile < num_tiles; tile++) { int tile_start = tile * KV_TILE; int tile_size = min(KV_TILE, N - tile_start); // Cooperative load of K tile const half2* k_tile_ptr2 = (const half2*)(k_ptr + tile_start * D); half2* k_smem2 = (half2*)smem_K; int k_elems = (tile_size * D + 1) / 2; for (int i = tid; i < k_elems; i += blockDim.x) { k_smem2[i] = k_tile_ptr2[i]; } // Cooperative load of V tile const half2* v_tile_ptr2 = (const half2*)(v_ptr + tile_start * D); half2* v_smem2 = (half2*)smem_V; int v_elems = (tile_size * D + 1) / 2; for (int i = tid; i < v_elems; i += blockDim.x) { v_smem2[i] = v_tile_ptr2[i]; } __syncthreads(); if (active) { int keys_needed = min(tile_size, qpos - tile_start + 1); if (keys_needed > 0) { for (int k = 0; k < keys_needed; k++) { const half2* k2_ptr = (const half2*)(smem_K + k * KV_PAD); half2 k2[DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2]; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2; i++) { k2[i] = k2_ptr[lane * (DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2) + i]; } float dot = 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2; i++) { float2 qf = __half22float2(q2[i]); float2 kf = __half22float2(k2[i]); dot += qf.x * kf.x + qf.y * kf.y; } dot = warp_reduce_sum(dot) * scale; float m_prev = m; m = fmaxf(m, dot); float exp_old = expf(m_prev - m); float exp_new = expf(dot - m); l = l * exp_old + exp_new; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD; i++) { o_acc[i] *= exp_old; } const half2* v2_ptr = (const half2*)(smem_V + k * KV_PAD); half2 v2[DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2]; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2; i++) { v2[i] = v2_ptr[lane * (DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2) + i]; float2 vf = __half22float2(v2[i]); o_acc[i * 2] += exp_new * vf.x; o_acc[i * 2 + 1] += exp_new * vf.y; } } } } __syncthreads(); } if (active) { float inv_l = 1.0f / l; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD; i++) { o_acc[i] *= inv_l; } half2* o2_ptr = (half2*)o_ptr; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2; i++) { half2 val; val.x = __float2half(o_acc[i * 2]); val.y = __float2half(o_acc[i * 2 + 1]); o2_ptr[lane * (DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2) + i] = val; } } } torch::Tensor attention_forward(torch::Tensor q, torch::Tensor k, torch::Tensor v) { int B = q.size(0); int Hq = q.size(1); int N = q.size(2); int D = q.size(3); int Hkv = k.size(1); auto out = torch::empty_like(q); const int ROWS_PER_BLOCK_VAL = 8; dim3 block(256); dim3 grid((N + ROWS_PER_BLOCK_VAL - 1) / ROWS_PER_BLOCK_VAL, Hq, B); float scale = 1.0f / sqrtf((float)D); gqa_attention_kernel<<<grid, block>>>( reinterpret_cast<const half*>(q.data_ptr<at::Half>()), reinterpret_cast<const half*>(k.data_ptr<at::Half>()), reinterpret_cast<const half*>(v.data_ptr<at::Half>()), reinterpret_cast<half*>(out.data_ptr<at::Half>()), B, Hq, Hkv, N, scale ); return out; } ''' class ModelNew(nn.Module): def __init__(self, num_heads, num_kv_heads, head_size): super().__init__() self.attn = load_inline( name="gqa_attention_h100", cpp_sources="", cuda_sources=CUDA_SOURCE, functions=["attention_forward"], extra_cuda_cflags=["-O3", "--use_fast_math", "-gencode=arch=compute_90,code=sm_90"], verbose=False, ) def forward(self, q, k, v): return self.attn.attention_forward(q, k, v) ``` Wait, there are several issues: 1. `load_inline` with `cpp_sources=""` might not work properly. Need to optimize for H100 (sm_90) One important issue with my shared memory access: ```cuda const half2* k2_ptr = (const half2*)(smem_K + k * KV_PAD); ``` Since `KV_PAD = 132`, each row of K in shared memory is 132 halfs. When we access `k2_ptr[lane * (DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2) + i]`, we're reading half2 values at offsets: - lane=0, i=0: offset 0 - lane=1, i=0: offset 2 - lane=2, i=0: offset 4 - ... - lane=31, i=0: offset 62 - lane=0, i=1: offset 64 Wait, DIMS_PER_THREAD = 4, so DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2 = 2. - lane=0: reads half2 at offsets 0, 2 - lane=1: reads half2 at offsets 4, 6 - etc. These are contiguous in memory: offsets 0, 2, 4, 6, 8, ... for the 32 threads. Since half2 is 32 bits, each thread reads 32 bits. The addresses are 0, 4, 8, 12, ... bytes. Bank for address A: (A / 4) % 32. - Thread 0: bank 0 - Thread 1: bank 1 - ... - Thread 31: bank 31 No conflicts! All threads access different banks. For row k and k+1 in shared memory: - k starts at k * KV_PAD * sizeof(half) bytes - k+1 starts at (k+1) * KV_PAD * sizeof(half) bytes - Difference = KV_PAD * 2 = 264 bytes - Bank for row k+1, col 0: (264 / 4) % 32 = 66 % 32 = 2 - Bank for row k, col 0: 0 - If two warps access row k and row k+1 simultaneously, threads in both warps that access the same column would hit different banks. Good! But wait, different warps in a block might access the same row k but different columns. For example, if two warps both happen to be processing the same k value at the same time. Within a block, each warp processes one query position and iterates over k sequentially. It's unlikely that two warps are at the exact same k at the same time, but possible if they have similar qpos. If two warps both access k and both read from `smem_K + k * KV_PAD`: - Warp 0 lane t: reads offset t * 4 - Warp 1 lane t: reads offset t * 4 - Bank for both: t - Bank conflict! Two warps accessing the same bank simultaneously. Hmm, this is a conflict between warps, not within a warp. Shared memory on H100 can service multiple warps, but if they access the same bank, there's serialization. To avoid this, I could process keys in a way that warps don't collide. For example, have warps process different keys within the same tile simultaneously. But since each warp independently iterates over keys 0..qpos, they naturally have some overlap. Actually, for a block handling positions 0..7: - Warp 0 (qpos=0): only processes k=0 - Warp 1 (qpos=1): processes k=0,1 - ... - Warp 7 (qpos=7): processes k=0..7 When warp 0 is at k=0, warps 1-7 might also be at k=0 (at the start of their loops). So 8 warps could simultaneously access k=0! This is a significant bank conflict. With 8 warps contending for the same banks, we get 8-way serialization. Solution: distribute the keys across warps. Instead of having each warp iterate k from 0 to qpos, we could: 1. Have each warp iterate, but the inner loop reads from shared memory and different warps might be at different k 2. Use a software pipeline where warps process keys at different offsets But this is complex. A simpler solution: don't use shared memory, and let each warp load K/V from L2 independently. Since L2 is so fast on H100, and each warp's loads are coalesced, register-only might be faster despite the redundant reads. Alternatively, I can try to stagger the warps' access patterns. For example, instead of warp i processing qpos=i, have warp i process qpos=i but start the loop differently. No, that doesn't help with the shared memory read pattern. Another solution: instead of having all warps read from the same shared memory arrays, partition shared memory per warp. Give each warp its own shared memory partition for loading K and V tiles. With 8 warps and 228KB shared memory per SM, each warp gets 28KB. But we also need to load tiles that all warps share... wait, if warps don't share K/V, then shared memory doesn't help and defeats the purpose. I think the best solution is to just use the register-only approach. No shared memory, no bank conflicts, much simpler code. For H100 with massive L2, this is viable and might be faster. Let me switch to the register-only approach. Register-only design: - Each warp handles one query position - Block handles 8 query positions - No shared memory - All loads from global/L2 Code for register-only: ```cuda #define WARP_SIZE 32 #define ROWS_PER_BLOCK 8 __device__ __forceinline__ float warp_reduce_sum(float val) { #pragma unroll for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset >>= 1) { val += __shfl_xor_sync(0xffffffff, val, offset); } return val; } __global__ void gqa_attention_kernel( const half* __restrict__ q, const half* __restrict__ k, const half* __restrict__ v, half* __restrict__ out, int B, int Hq, int Hkv, int N, int D, float scale ) { int tid = threadIdx.x; int warp_id = tid / WARP_SIZE; int lane = tid % WARP_SIZE; int head = blockIdx.y; int batch = blockIdx.z; int seq_tile = blockIdx.x; int kv_head = head * Hkv / Hq; int qpos = seq_tile * ROWS_PER_BLOCK + warp_id; if (qpos >= N) return; const half* q_ptr = q + (((int64_t)batch * Hq + head) * N + qpos) * D; const half* k_ptr = k + (((int64_t)batch * Hkv + kv_head) * N) * D; const half* v_ptr = v + (((int64_t)batch * Hkv + kv_head) * N) * D; half* o_ptr = out + (((int64_t)batch * Hq + head) * N + qpos) * D; int dims_per_thread = D / WARP_SIZE; // Load query into registers half2 q2[64 / WARP_SIZE]; // dims_per_thread / 2 half2s const half2* q2_ptr = (const half2*)q_ptr; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < dims_per_thread / 2; i++) { q2[i] = q2_ptr[lane * (dims_per_thread / 2) + i]; } float o_acc[4]; // dims_per_thread floats #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < dims_per_thread; i++) o_acc[i] = 0.0f; float m = -INFINITY; float l = 0.0f; // Iterate over all keys up to qpos for (int key = 0; key <= qpos; key++) { // Load K[key, :] slice const half2* k2_ptr = (const half2*)(k_ptr + key * D); half2 k2[dims_per_thread / 2]; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < dims_per_thread / 2; i++) { k2[i] = k2_ptr[lane * (dims_per_thread / 2) + i]; } // Compute dot product float dot = 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < dims_per_thread / 2; i++) { float2 qf = __half22float2(q2[i]); float2 kf = __half22float2(k2[i]); dot += qf.x * kf.x + qf.y * kf.y; } dot = warp_reduce_sum(dot) * scale; // Online softmax float m_prev = m; m = fmaxf(m, dot); float exp_old = expf(m_prev - m); float exp_new = expf(dot - m); l = l * exp_old + exp_new; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < dims_per_thread; i++) { o_acc[i] *= exp_old; } // Load V and accumulate const half2* v2_ptr = (const half2*)(v_ptr + key * D); half2 v2[dims_per_thread / 2]; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < dims_per_thread / 2; i++) { v2[i] = v2_ptr[lane * (dims_per_thread / 2) + i]; float2 vf = __half22float2(v2[i]); o_acc[i * 2] += exp_new * vf.x; o_acc[i * 2 + 1] += exp_new * vf.y; } } // Normalize and write float inv_l = 1.0f / l; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < dims_per_thread; i++) { o_acc[i] *= inv_l; } half2* o2_ptr = (half2*)o_ptr; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < dims_per_thread / 2; i++) { half2 val; val.x = __float2half(o_acc[i * 2]); val.y = __float2half(o_acc[i * 2 + 1]); o2_ptr[lane * (dims_per_thread / 2) + i] = val; } } ``` Wait, but `dims_per_thread` is only known at runtime (though in this problem it's fixed to 4). If I make it a template parameter, I can unroll properly. Let me template on D: ```cuda template<int D> __global__ void gqa_attention_kernel(...) { static_assert(D % WARP_SIZE == 0, "D must be divisible by WARP_SIZE"); constexpr int DIMS_PER_THREAD = D / WARP_SIZE; ... } ``` For D=128, DIMS_PER_THREAD=4. The arrays `q2[2]`, `k2[2]`, `v2[2]`, `o_acc[4]` are all small and fit in registers. With this register-only approach, each warp computes one query position. For 8 warps per block and 16384 blocks, total parallelism is high. But we still have the issue with `return` inside `if (qpos >= N)`. If some warps in a block return and others don't, the block might not synchronize properly. But in this register-only kernel, there are no `__syncthreads()`, so returning early is fine! Other warps in the block continue independently. This is a key advantage of the register-only approach: no need for `__syncthreads()` and no deadlock risk from early returns. Wait, but if a warp returns early, its thread slots are freed. The other warps in the block continue. This is fine on modern GPUs. However, if we want to avoid the early return for cleaner code, we can structure it as: ```cuda if (qpos >= N) { // Do nothing but fall through to the end } else { // Compute attention } ``` This avoids any return statements. But a return in a warp is fine when there are no __syncthreads(). Actually, CUDA doesn't allow `return` to cause divergence issues when there are no synchreads. Each warp is scheduled independently. So `if (qpos >= N) return;` is perfectly safe when there are no `__syncthreads()`. OK, I'm going with the register-only approach. It's simpler, avoids shared memory bank conflicts, and should perform well on H100's L2. One more optimization: I should use `__ldg()` for read-only global memory loads. `__ldg()` uses the texture cache / read-only cache, which can help for K and V reads. On H100, `__ldg()` is a hint to use the L1/texture cache path. Actually, on modern GPUs (Volta+), regular loads to read-only data already go through the same path if the compiler can prove read-only. Using `const __restrict__` helps. `__ldg()` is an explicit hint. Let me add `__ldg()` for K and V loads. For Q, it's loaded once so it doesn't matter much. ```cuda k2[i] = __ldg(&k2_ptr[lane * (dims_per_thread / 2) + i]); ``` Hmm, but as I'm accessing K/V repeatedly in a loop, the compiler might already optimize this as cached loads. Let me just rely on `const __restrict__` which I've already used. One more thought: for H100, we can enable more aggressive optimizations with compiler flags: - `-O3` - `--use_fast_math` (enables approximate math functions, including faster expf) - `-gencode=arch=compute_90,code=sm_90` These should all be in `extra_cuda_cflags`. The flag `--use_fast_math` makes `expf` faster but potentially less accurate. For attention, `expf` accuracy is important near 0. But the tolerance for fp16 is 1e-2, so approximate expf should be fine. Actually, FlashAttention uses `expf` with online softmax. The exact vs approximate exp might give slightly different results, but within fp16 tolerance it should be OK. Let me be safe and not use `--use_fast_math` if I'm worried about accuracy. Actually, let me use it and see if correctness passes. We can always remove it. Now, I should also template the kernel on D to allow unrolling. The I/O contract says HEAD_SIZE=128, so I can specialize for D=128. But I might want to support any D that's a multiple of WARP_SIZE and 2. For safety, I'll template on D and dispatch in the launcher. Or just hardcode D=128 since that's in the contract. Actually, looking at the inputs: `HEAD_SIZE = 128` is a constant in the problem. The hidden reference only tests with this size. So I can hardcode D=128. But to make the code cleaner and more general, I'll use a template. Wait, there's another issue. The I/O contract has: ```python q = torch.randn(BATCH, NUM_HEADS, SEQ_LEN, HEAD_SIZE, dtype=torch.float16).cuda() ``` So q is shape [4, 32, 1024, 128]. This is batch-first. My indexing: `q + (((int64_t)batch * Hq + head) * N + qpos) * D` For batch=0, head=0, qpos=0: offset = 0. Good. For batch=0, head=0, qpos=1: offset = D = 128 halfs. Good. For batch=0, head=1, qpos=0: offset = N * D = 1024 * 128 = 131072 halfs. Good. This assumes contiguous memory layout, which is the default for torch.randn. One more thing: in the problem, `k` and `v` have shape [BATCH, NUM_KV_HEADS, SEQ_LEN, HEAD_SIZE]. So k_stride_0 = Hkv * N * D, k_stride_1 = N * D. My k indexing: `k + (((int64_t)batch * Hkv + kv_head) * N + key) * D` This assumes contiguous layout. For the standard layout, this is correct. Let me now also think about about whether I should add some small epsilon to the softmax sum to avoid division by zero. If all scores are -inf (which shouldn't happen for causal attention with at least k=0), we could get l=0. But for qpos >= 0 and at least one key (k=0), we always have at least one valid score, so l > 0. OK, I'm going to write and compile the code now. Let me double check my calculations: - BATCH=4, NUM_HEADS=32, NUM_KV_HEADS=8, HEAD_SIZE=128, SEQ_LEN=1024 - Grid: (1024/8=128, 32, 4) = 16384 blocks - Block: 256 threads - Each block: 8 warps, each handles one query position - Total warps: 16384 * 8 = 131072 warps - Total query positions: 4 * 32 * 1024 = 131072 Wait, 128 blocks in x-dimension, 32 in y, 4 in z = 16384 blocks. Each block handles 8 query positions → 131072 query positions. Perfect match. Each warp handles one query position. So there's a 1:1 mapping between warps and query positions. Excellent. Register usage estimate: - q2[2] = 2 half2 = 2 registers (32-bit each) - k2[2] = 2 registers - v2[2] = 2 registers - o_acc[4] = 4 float = 4 registers - dot, m, l, inv_l, key, etc. = ~15 registers - Total: ~25 32-bit registers With 25 registers per thread, 256 threads = 6400 registers per block. 64K per SM means we can easily fit 8-10 blocks per SM. But max threads per SM is 2048, so max 8 blocks of 256 threads. Register pressure is not a limiting factor. Now about grid launch overhead: 16384 blocks is a lot. H100 has 132 SMs. 16384 / 132 ≈ 124 blocks per SM. This is good for saturating the GPU. Let me write the code now and compile it.

callcompile_kernel { "kernel_code": "\nimport torch\nimport torch.nn as nn\nfrom torch.utils.cpp_extension import load_inline\nimport math\n\nCUDA_SOURCE = r'''\n#include <torch/extension.h>\n#include <cuda_runtime.h>\n#include <cuda_fp16.h>\n#include <float.h>\n\n#define WARP_SIZE 32\n#define ROWS_PER_BLOCK 4\n\ntemplate <int D>\n__device__ __forceinline__ float warp_reduce_sum(float val) {\n #pragma unroll\n for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset >>= 1) {\n val += __shfl_xor_sync(0xffffffff, val, offset);\n }\n return val;\n}\n\ntemplate <int D>\n__device__ __forceinline__ float warp_reduce_max(float val) {\n #pragma unroll\n for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset >>= 1) {\n val = fmaxf(val, __shfl_xor_sync(0xffffffff, val, offset));\n }\n return val;\n}\n\ntemplate <int D>\n__launch_bounds__(128) \n__global__ void gqa_attention_kernel(\n const half* __restrict__ q,\n const half* __restrict__ k,\n const half* __restrict__ v,\n half* __restrict__ out,\n int B, int Hq, int Hkv, int N, float scale\n) {\n int tid = threadIdx.x;\n int warp_id = tid / WARP_SIZE;\n int lane = tid % WARP_SIZE;\n \n int head = blockIdx.y;\n int batch = blockIdx.z;\n int seq_tile = blockIdx.x;\n \n int kv_head = head * Hkv / Hq;\n int qpos = seq_tile * ROWS_PER_BLOCK + warp_id;\n \n if (qpos >= N) return;\n \n const half* q_ptr = q + (((int64_t)batch * Hq + head) * N + qpos) * D;\n const half* k_ptr = k + (((int64_t)batch * Hkv + kv_head) * N) * D;\n const half* v_ptr = v + (((int64_t)batch * Hkv + kv_head) * N) * D;\n half* o_ptr = out + (((int64_t)batch * Hq + head) * N + qpos) * D;\n \n constexpr int DIMS_PER_THREAD = D / WARP_SIZE;\n \n // Load query into registers - use vectorized loads\n half2 q2[DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2];\n const half2* q2_ptr = (const half2*)q_ptr;\n #pragma unroll\n for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2; i++) {\n q2[i] = q2_ptr[lane * (DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2) + i];\n }\n \n // Output accumulator in float\n float o_acc[DIMS_PER_THREAD];\n #pragma unroll\n for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD; i++) o_acc[i] = 0.0f;\n \n float m = -INFINITY;\n float l = 0.0f;\n \n // Iterate over keys\n #pragma unroll 4\n for (int key = 0; key <= qpos; key++) {\n const half2* k2_ptr = (const half2*)(k_ptr + key * D);\n half2 k2[DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2];\n #pragma unroll\n for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2; i++) {\n k2[i] = k2_ptr[lane * (DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2) + i];\n }\n \n // Compute dot product Q@K\n float dot = 0.0f;\n #pragma unroll\n for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2; i++) {\n float2 qf = __half22float2(q2[i]);\n float2 kf = __half22float2(k2[i]);\n dot += qf.x * kf.x + qf.y * kf.y;\n }\n dot = warp_reduce_sum<D>(dot) * scale;\n \n // Online softmax update\n float m_prev = m;\n m = fmaxf(m, dot);\n float exp_old = expf(m_prev - m);\n float exp_new = expf(dot - m);\n l = l * exp_old + exp_new;\n \n #pragma unroll\n for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD; i++) {\n o_acc[i] *= exp_old;\n }\n \n // Load V and accumulate\n const half2* v2_ptr = (const half2*)(v_ptr + key * D);\n half2 v2[DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2];\n #pragma unroll\n for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2; i++) {\n v2[i] = v2_ptr[lane * (DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2) + i];\n float2 vf = __half22float2(v2[i]);\n o_acc[i * 2] += exp_new * vf.x;\n o_acc[i * 2 + 1] += exp_new * vf.y;\n }\n }\n \n float inv_l = 1.0f / l;\n #pragma unroll\n for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD; i++) {\n o_acc[i] *= inv_l;\n }\n \n // Store result\n half2* o2_ptr = (half2*)o_ptr;\n #pragma unroll\n for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2; i++) {\n half2 val;\n val.x = __float2half_rn(o_acc[i * 2]);\n val.y = __float2half_rn(o_acc[i * 2 + 1]);\n o2_ptr[lane * (DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2) + i] = val;\n }\n}\n\ntorch::Tensor attention_forward(torch::Tensor q, torch::Tensor k, torch::Tensor v) {\n int B = q.size(0);\n int Hq = q.size(1);\n int N = q.size(2);\n int D = q.size(3);\n int Hkv = k.size(1);\n \n auto out = torch::empty_like(q);\n \n dim3 block(128);\n dim3 grid((N + ROWS_PER_BLOCK - 1) / ROWS_PER_BLOCK, Hq, B);\n \n float scale = 1.0f / sqrtf((float)D);\n \n switch (D) {\n case 128:\n gqa_attention_kernel<128><<<grid, block>>>(\n reinterpret_cast<const half*>(q.data_ptr<at::Half>()),\n reinterpret_cast<const half*>(k.data_ptr<at::Half>()),\n reinterpret_cast<const half*>(v.data_ptr<at::Half>()),\n reinterpret_cast<half*>(out.data_ptr<at::Half>()),\n B, Hq, Hkv, N, scale\n );\n break;\n default:\n TORCH_CHECK(false, \"Unsupported head size: \", D);\n }\n \n return out;\n}\n'''\n\ncpp_source = r'''\ntorch::Tensor attention_forward(torch::Tensor q, torch::Tensor k, torch::Tensor v);\n'''\n\nclass ModelNew(nn.Module):\n def __init__(self, num_heads, num_kv_heads, head_size):\n super().__init__()\n self.num_heads = num_heads\n self.num_kv_heads = num_kv_heads\n self.head_size = head_size\n self._attention = load_inline(\n name=\"gqa_attention_h100_v2\",\n cpp_sources=cpp_source,\n cuda_sources=CUDA_SOURCE,\n functions=[\"attention_forward\"],\n extra_cuda_cflags=[\"-O3\", \"--use_fast_math\", \"-gencode=arch=compute_90,code=sm_90\"],\n verbose=False,\n )\n\n def forward(self, q, k, v):\n return self._attention.attention_forward(q, k, v)\n" }

This creates divergence. A cleaner approach: have each block handle a contiguous range of queries and keys. Process the attention matrix in tiles where within each tile, we guarantee all queries can access all keys in the tile. For causal attention, we can use the standard FlashAttention approach: divide the attention matrix into blocks, and for diagonal blocks, use causal masking. For below-diagonal blocks, no masking needed. Specifically: - Tile the sequence into blocks of size Bs - For block (i, j) where i is query block and j is key block: - If j < i: full attention (no masking) - If j == i: causal masking (only upper triangular within block) - If j > i: skip (causal) This requires each block to handle Bs queries against Bs keys. We can use shared memory efficiently. Let me implement this FlashAttention-style kernel for H100: Block configuration: - Bs = 64 (block size) - Each block handles a [Bs, Bs] tile of attention - Threads: 128 or 256 For j < i tiles (full tiles): - Load K tile and V tile into shared memory - Each thread computes multiple query-key dot products - Compute softmax online - Accumulate output For j == i tiles (causal tiles): - Same but with masking This is getting complex but doable. Let me write a simpler version since seq_len is small (1024). Actually, let me try something even simpler. For B=4, H=32, N=1024, the total number of attention matrices is 128. Each is 1024x1024. We have 132 SMs. I'll implement a kernel where: - 1 block per (batch, head, query_tile) - Each block handles BLOCK_Q query positions - Each block loads KEY_TILE keys at a time into shared memory - All threads in block cooperate on loading and computing Let me try BLOCK_Q=16 and KEY_TILE=64 with 128 threads: - 16 queries processed per block - Shared memory: K tile 64*128*2=16KB, V tile 16KB = 32KB total - Each query gets 128/16 = 8 threads? Or we organize by dimensions. Each warp loads K elements from shared memory (4 half2s per key per warp due to 128 dims / 32 lanes = 4 dims per lane = 2 half2s). Actually with dims=128 and 32 lanes, each lane handles 4 dims = 2 half2s. Wait, let me be more careful: each warp has 32 threads. D=128. So each thread handles 128/32=4 dims = 2 half2s. For each key in the tile, a warp loads 2 half2s from shared memory per thread = 64 half2s per warp. For KEY_TILE=64 keys, each warp loads 64*64=4096 half2s from shared memory. This is a lot of shared memory bandwidth. A better approach: have more threads per query to parallelize the reduction, or use a different organization. Actually, for compute efficiency, we want each thread to do more FMAs. With the dot product over 128 dims, each thread does 4 FMAs (2 per half2, 2 half2s), and then we do a warp reduction. That's only 4 FMAs per key. With 1024 keys, that's 4096 FMAs per query, which is tiny. For H100, we need about 10-100x more FMAs to hide memory latency. So we need to either: 1. Maybe I should just write a matmul-based attention that computes K^T first (or loads it), then does Q@K^T using shared memory, then softmax, then @V. For causal attention with N=1024: 1. Alright, let me just write the tiled version with shared memory and see if it's better: ```cuda template<int D, int Q_TILE, int KV_TILE> __global__ void causal_attention_tiled_kernel(...) { int tid = threadIdx.x; i++) o_acc[i] = 0.0f;\n \n float m = -INFINITY;\n float l = 0.0f;\n \n // Iterate over keys\n #pragma unroll 4\n for (int key = 0; key <= qpos; key++) {\n const half2* k2_ptr = (const half2*)(k_ptr + key * D);\n half2 k2[DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2];\n #pragma unroll\n for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2; i++) {\n k2[i] = k2_ptr[lane * (DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2) + i];\n }\n \n // Compute dot product Q@K\n float dot = 0.0f;\n #pragma unroll\n for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2; i++) {\n float2 qf = __half22float2(q2[i]);\n float2 kf = __half22float2(k2[i]);\n dot += qf.x * kf.x + qf.y * kf.y;\n }\n dot = warp_reduce_sum<D>(dot) * scale;\n \n // Online softmax update\n float m_prev = m;\n m = fmaxf(m, dot);\n float exp_old = expf(m_prev - m);\n float exp_new = expf(dot - m);\n l = l * exp_old + exp_new;\n \n #pragma unroll\n for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD; i++) {\n o_acc[i] *= exp_old;\n }\n \n // Load V and accumulate\n const half2* v2_ptr = (const half2*)(v_ptr + key * D);\n half2 v2[DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2];\n #pragma unroll\n for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2; i++) {\n v2[i] = v2_ptr[lane * (DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2) + i];\n float2 vf = __half22float2(v2[i]);\n o_acc[i * 2] += exp_new * vf.x;\n o_acc[i * 2 + 1] += exp_new * vf.y;\n }\n }\n \n float inv_l = 1.0f / l;\n #pragma unroll\n for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD; i++) {\n o_acc[i] *= inv_l;\n }\n \n // Store result\n half2* o2_ptr = (half2*)o_ptr;\n #pragma unroll\n for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2; i++) {\n half2 val;\n val.x = __float2half_rn(o_acc[i * 2]);\n val.y = __float2half_rn(o_acc[i * 2 + 1]);\n o2_ptr[lane * (DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2) + i] = val;\n }\n}\n\ntorch::Tensor attention_forward(torch::Tensor q, torch::Tensor k, torch::Tensor v) {\n int B = q.size(0);\n int Hq = q.size(1);\n int N = q.size(2);\n int D = q.size(3);\n int Hkv = k.size(1);\n \n auto out = torch::empty_like(q);\n \n dim3 block(128);\n dim3 grid((N + ROWS_PER_BLOCK - 1) / ROWS_PER_BLOCK, Hq, B);\n \n float scale = 1.0f / sqrtf((float)D);\n \n switch (D) {\n case 128:\n gqa_attention_kernel<128><<<grid, block>>>(\n reinterpret_cast<const half*>(q.data_ptr<at::Half>()),\n reinterpret_cast<const half*>(k.data_ptr<at::Half>()),\n reinterpret_cast<const half*>(v.data_ptr<at::Half>()),\n reinterpret_cast<half*>(out.data_ptr<at::Half>()),\n B, Hq, Hkv, N, scale\n );\n break;\n default:\n TORCH_CHECK(false, \"Unsupported head size: \", D);\n }\n \n return out;\n}\n'''\n\ncpp_source = r'''\ntorch::Tensor attention_forward(torch::Tensor q, torch::Tensor k, torch::Tensor v);\n'''\n\nclass ModelNew(nn.Module):\n def __init__(self, num_heads, num_kv_heads, head_size):\n super().__init__()\n self.num_heads = num_heads\n self.num_kv_heads = num_kv_heads\n self.head_size = head_size\n self._attention = load_inline(\n name=\"gqa_attention_h100_v2\",\n cpp_sources=cpp_source,\n cuda_sources=CUDA_SOURCE,\n functions=[\"attention_forward\"],\n extra_cuda_cflags=[\"-O3\", \"--use_fast_math\", \"-gencode=arch=compute_90,code=sm_90\"],\n verbose=False,\n )\n\n def forward(self, q, k, v):\n return self._attention.attention_forward(q, k, v)\n" }

Let me write a SM-tiled version for the H100 with cooperative loading: ```cuda #include <torch/extension.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> #include <cuda_fp16.h> #include <float.h> #define WARP_SIZE 32 #define TILE_K 64 // tile size over key dimension #define ROWS_PER_BLOCK 8 template <int D> __device__ __forceinline__ float warp_reduce_sum(float val) { #pragma unroll for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset >>= 1) { val += __shfl_xor_sync(0xffffffff, val, offset); } return val; } template <int D> __launch_bounds__(256, 2) __global__ void gqa_attention_kernel( const half* __restrict__ q, const half* __restrict__ k, const half* __restrict__ v, half* __restrict__ out, int B, int Hq, int Hkv, int N, float scale ) { extern __shared__ half smem[]; half* smem_k = smem; // TILE_K * D elements half* smem_v = smem + TILE_K * D; // TILE_K * D elements // Total shared memory: 2 * TILE_K * D * sizeof(half) = 2 * 64 * 128 * 2 = 32KB int tid = threadIdx.x; int warp_id = tid / WARP_SIZE; int lane = tid % WARP_SIZE; int head = blockIdx.y; int batch = blockIdx.z; int seq_tile = blockIdx.x; int kv_head = head * Hkv / Hq; int qpos = seq_tile * ROWS_PER_BLOCK + warp_id; if (qpos >= N) return; const half* q_ptr = q + (((int64_t)batch * Hq + head) * N + qpos) * D; const half* k_ptr = k + (((int64_t)batch * Hkv + kv_head) * N) * D; const half* v_ptr = v + (((int64_t)batch * Hkv + kv_head) * N) * D; half* o_ptr = out + (((int64_t)batch * Hq + head) * N + qpos) * D; constexpr int DIMS_PER_THREAD = D / WARP_SIZE; // Load query into registers half2 q2[DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2]; const half2* q2_ptr = (const half2*)q_ptr; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2; i++) { q2[i] = q2_ptr[lane * (DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2) + i]; } float o_acc[DIMS_PER_THREAD]; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD; i++) o_acc[i] = 0.0f; float m = -INFINITY; float l = 0.0f; // Iterate over key tiles for (int kstart = 0; kstart <= qpos; kstart += TILE_K) { int kend = min(kstart + TILE_K, qpos + 1); int key_count = kend - kstart; // Load K tile into shared memory cooperatively for (int idx = tid; idx < key_count * D; idx += blockDim.x) { int k_idx = kstart + idx / D; int d_idx = idx % D; smem_k[idx] = k_ptr[k_idx * D + d_idx]; } __syncthreads(); // Compute QK dot products for each key in tile for (int koffset = 0; koffset < key_count; koffset++) { half2 k2[DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2]; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2; i++) { k2[i] = ((half2*)(smem_k + koffset * D))[lane * (DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2) + i]; } float dot = 0.0f; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2; i++) { float2 qf = __half22float2(q2[i]); float2 kf = __half22float2(k2[i]); dot += qf.x * kf.x + qf.y * kf.y; } dot = warp_reduce_sum<D>(dot) * scale; float m_prev = m; m = fmaxf(m, dot); float exp_old = expf(m_prev - m); float exp_new = expf(dot - m); l = l * exp_old + exp_new; #pragma unroll for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD; i++) { o_acc[i] *= exp_old; } // Need V for accumulation // Since we haven't loaded V yet, compute QK first for all keys, then load V // Actually we need V synchronously for online softmax } // We need V synchronously, so load V tile too for (int idx = tid; idx < key_count * D; idx += blockDim.x) { int k_idx = kstart + idx / D; int d_idx = idx % D; smem_v[idx] = v_ptr[k_idx * D + d_idx]; } __syncthreads(); // Now recompute using V tile // Wait, this is wasteful. We should interleave QK and V. } } ``` This is getting complicated with online softmax because each key's V must be available immediately after computing its dot product. For online softmax, we can't precompute all QK dot products then load V afterwards. We need V at the same time. So it's better to load BOTH K and V tiles into shared memory simultaneously. Option 2: Process K/V tiles in batches and let threads accumulate partial sums. Proper synchronization Here's the implementation sketch: ```cpp template <int D, int TILE_K> __global__ void attention_tiled(...) { // shared memory: K_tile[TILE_K][D] and V_tile[TILE_K][D] in half // Or float? No, keep as half to save SMEM. extern __shared__ half smem[]; half* k_smem = smem; half* v_smem = smem + TILE_K * D; int warp_id = threadIdx.x / 32; int lane = threadIdx.x % 32; constexpr int WARPS = 256 / 32; // 8 int row = blockIdx.x * WARPS + warp_id; if (row >= N) return; // Load query ... float m = -INFINITY, l = 0; float o_acc[DIMS_PER_THREAD] = {0}; int num_keys = row + 1; // causal int num_tiles = (num_keys + TILE_K - 1) / TILE_K; for (int tile = 0; tile < num_tiles; tile++) { int k_start = tile * TILE_K; int k_end = min(k_start + TILE_K, num_keys); int keys_in_tile = k_end - k_start; // Load K and V for this tile cooperatively int total_halfs = 2 * keys_in_tile * D; for (int idx = threadIdx.x; idx < total_halfs; idx += 256) { int is_v = idx >= keys_in_tile * D; int local_idx = is_v ? (idx - keys_in_tile * D) : idx; int key = local_idx / D + k_start; int dim = local_idx % D; if (!is_v) k_smem[local_idx] = k[(key * D + dim)]; else v_smem[local_idx] = v[(key * D + dim)]; } __syncthreads(); // Each warp processes its row using the tile data for (int k = k_start; k < k_end; k++) { // Load K[k] from shared memory // Load V[k] from shared memory // compute dot and accumulate... } __syncthreads(); } // write output } ``` This is pretty elegant! Let me implement this! I'll use TILE_K=64 for H100 (shared memory is 228KB, we can afford 32KB per block). Wait, but there's a subtlety with shared memory bank conflicts. If threads load `smem[key * D + dim]`, and within a warp, each thread accesses a different `dim` but same `key`, we get: - Each thread reads `smem[key * D + lane * dims_per_thread + i]` - `key * D` is the same for all threads in a warp - `lane * dims_per_thread` gives strided accesses - For half2, each thread reads `lane * 4 + i` etc. If `D % 32 == 0` and D=128, then `key * 128 + lane * 4` gives stride of 4 halfs = 8 bytes. Shared memory is organized into 32 banks. With 8-byte access per thread, consecutive threads access banks (lane * 4 / 2) % 32 = (lane * 2) % 32. Since 2 and 32 are not coprime, threads 0 and 16 access the same bank! This could cause 2-way bank conflicts. To avoid bank conflicts, we should pad the shared memory layout or transpose. Option: Store K in transposed order: `smem[dim][key]` instead of `smem[key][dim]`. Then warp lane `lid` reads key data at `smem[dim][key]` for different dims across threads. But all threads in the warp read the same `key`, so consecutive threads read `smem[dim_0][key]`, `smem[dim_1][key]`, etc. If `dim_0, dim_1, ...` are consecutive, and each `smem[dim]` is at a different bank, this is conflict-free! For half2, we read two consecutive dims. If we store by half, `smem[dim * TILE_K + key]`, then thread `lane` reads at `dim = lane * dims_per_thread`, which means `(lane * 4) * TILE_K + key`. Bank = `(lane * 4 * TILE_K / 2) % 32`. For TILE_K=64: `(lane * 128) % 32 = 0`. Bank conflict! All threads access the same bank. So transposed doesn't work either for this layout. We need to swizzle or pad. For `smem[key][D]`, storing with D padded to multiple of 64 (or whatever): `smem[key * padded_D + dim]` If `padded_D = 132` (next multiple of... actually we want padded_D % 32 to provide good distribution). Actually, for half2 access at `smem[key * padded_D + lane * 4]`, bank = `(key * padded_D + lane * 4) / 2 % 32 = (key * padded_D / 2 + lane * 2) % 32`. If `padded_D / 2` is odd, then `key * odd` gives different banks for different keys, but threads in a warp have lane * 2 which causes 2-way conflicts (threads 0 and 16 same bank). To avoid ALL conflicts, we'd need `padded_D / 2` to be coprime with 32 AND `lane * 2` not to conflict. But `lane * 2` always has gcd 2 with 32, so we always get 2-way conflicts for this access pattern. Wait, is this true? Let me think about shared memory banks on H100. H100 shared memory has 32 banks. Each bank holds 8 bytes per address. For half2 (4 bytes), two half2 values fit in one bank address. When we access `smem[idx]`, the bank is `(idx * sizeof(half)) / 8 % 32`. For half2 access: `sizeof(half2) = 4`. Bank = `(idx * 2) / 8 % 32`? Let me use the byte address: `bank = (byte_addr / 8) % 32`. For half2 at element index `idx` (in halfs), the byte address is `idx * 2`. So `bank = (idx * 2 / 8) % 32 = (idx / 4) % 32`. Thread `lane` accesses `idx = key * D + lane * 4 + offset`. Bank = `(key * D + lane * 4 + offset) / 4 % 32 = (key * D / 4 + lane + offset / 4) % 32`. For D = 128: `D / 4 = 32`. Bank = `(key * 32 + lane + offset / 4) % 32 = (lane + offset / 4) % 32`. This is PERFECT! No bank conflicts at all! Consecutive lanes access consecutive banks. Wait, is this right? If D = 128 halfs, and we store as `smem[key * D + dim]`, then for thread `lane` reading `dim = lane * 4` (for dims_per_thread=4): `idx = key * 128 + lane * 4`. Bank in half2 elements: wait, let me recalculate. Actually, let's stick to bytes. Base pointer is `half* smem`. For element at `smem[i]`, byte address is `(char*)&smem[i] - (char*)smem = i * 2`. Bank = `(i * 2 / 8) % 32 = (i / 4) % 32` (integer division). For `i = key * D + dim`, where D=128 and `dim = lane * 4 + j`: `i = key * 128 + lane * 4 + j`. Bank = `(key * 128 + lane * 4 + j) / 4 % 32 = (key * 32 + lane + j / 4) % 32`. For j = 0..3: - j = 0: bank = (key * 32 + lane) % 32 = lane % 32 (no conflict) - j = 1: bank = (key * 32 + lane) % 32 (if j < 4, floor(j/4) = 0) Wait, `j / 4` as integer division is 0 for j = 0, 1, 2, 3. So all j give the same bank? No, because each thread loads in half2 units at `dim = lane * 4`, which corresponds to `j = 0` and `j = 1` being loaded as one half2. The second half2 would be at `j = 2`. Actually, let me simplify: if each thread reads a half2 at `dim = lane * 4`, then the starting index is `key * 128 + lane * 4`. This is a contiguous 64-element (128-byte) access across the warp. Bank = `(key * 32 + lane) % 32`. Perfect distribution, no conflicts! For the next half2 at `dim = lane * 4 + 2`: bank = `(key * 32 + lane) % 32` again. Still no conflicts! So there's no bank conflict for K! Similarly for V! This is because D=128, which is a multiple of 4 (the coalescing size for half2) and `D/4 = 32` which is coprime with the bank count in terms of distribution. Actually D/4 mod 32 = 0, which means each key row starts at bank 0. But consecutive lanes access consecutive banks within the row. So for `bank = (key * 32 + lane) % 32 = lane % 32`. Each lane has a unique bank. Perfect! For V it's the same. What about storing both K and V? If we store K at offset 0 (length TILE_K * 128 halfs) and V right after, then V starts at index TILE_K * 128. Bank for V[index] = `((TILE_K * 128 + index) / 4) % 32 = (TILE_K * 32 + index / 4) % 32 = (index / 4) % 32` (since TILE_K = 32 or 64, TILE_K * 32 is multiple of 32). So V also has no bank conflicts! This is excellent. Shared memory layout works perfectly without padding. Let me implement the tiled kernel. Actually wait, I want to double check for TILE_K values. If TILE_K = 32: K is 32 * 128 = 4096 halfs. V starts at 4096. For any key in K: `i_key = key * 128 + dim`. For any key in V: `i_key_v = 4096 + key * 128 + dim`. Bank_K = `(key * 32 + dim / 4) % 32`. Bank_V = `(1024 + key * 32 + dim / 4) % 32 = (key * 32 + dim / 4) % 32`. Same perfect distribution. OK great, so `smem[key * D + dim]` with D=128 works perfectly for both K and V, no padding needed. Now, for loading data into shared memory: We need to load K and V for keys `k_start` to `k_end`. The size is `num_keys * D` halfs. For K: load from global `k_ptr + k_start * D`. For V: load from global `v_ptr + k_start * D`. Total elements to load: `2 * num_keys * D` halfs. With 256 threads, each thread loads `ceil(2 * num_keys * D / 256)` halfs. For TILE_K=64: `2 * 64 * 128 / 256 = 64` halfs per thread. That's 32 half2s per thread, or 32 vectorized loads. This is a lot of loads and may dominate the latency. For TILE_K=32: `2 * 32 * 128 / 256 = 32` halfs per thread = 16 half2 loads. More reasonable. Let's try TILE_K=32. For late blocks, they need many tiles, but each tile is small. Number of tiles for block b (start 8b) is ceil((8b+8)/32). Total tiles loaded = sum ceil((8b+8)/32) over b=0..127. We calculated this earlier: for TILE_K=32, total K loads = 67,584 per head. Without tiling: 524,288. Reduction: 7.76x. For TILE_K=64: Block b needs ceil((8b+8)/64) tiles. Total K loads = 64 * sum ceil((b+1)/8) from b=0..127 = 64 * [8 * (1+2+...+16) + sum of partials for ceil=17] = actually let me just calculate: For b=0..7: ceil((b+1)/8) = 1. Total = 8. For b=8..15: ceil = 2. Total = 16. For b=16..23: ceil = 3. Total = 24. ... For b=120..127: ceil((121..128)/8) = ceil(15.125..16) = 16. Total = 8 * (1+2+...+16) = 8 * 16 * 17 / 2 = 1088. Total loads = 64 * 1088 = 69,632. Actually slightly more than TILE_K=32! So smaller TILE_K is better for early rows, and similar for late rows. What about TILE_K=16? Total = 16 * sum ceil((b+1)/2) from b=0..127 = 16 * [2 * (1+2+...+64)] Wait: b=0: ceil(1/2) = 1. Actually ceil(0.5) = 1. b=0..1: ceil(1/2..2/2) = ceil(0.5..1) = all 1 for b=0, 1 for b=1. = 2 * 1 = 2 b=2..3: ceil(3/2..4/2) = ceil(1.5..2) = all 2. = 4 * 2 = wait no. Actually: b=0,1: ceil((b+1)/2) = 1. Sum = 2. b=2,3: ceil = 2. Sum = 4. ... b=126,127: ceil((127..128)/2) = 64. Sum = 128. Total sum = 2 + 4 + 6 + ... + 128 = 2 * (1 + 2 + ... + 64) = 2 * 64 * 65 / 2 = 4160. Total loads = 16 * 4160 = 66,560. Better than 32! So smaller tiles are better for covering the triangular pattern. But with TILE_K=16 and 8 warps per block, the loading overhead might be high because we need more tiles and thus more __syncthreads. For TILE_K=8: Sum ceil((b+1)/1) from b=0..127 = 1 + 2 + ... + 128 = 128 * 129 / 2 = 8256. Total loads = 8 * 8256 = 66,048. Smaller tiles give marginally better coverage of the triangular pattern, but the overhead of more __syncthreads and tile iterations could negate the benefit. For TILE_K=16: - Each thread loads 2 * 16 * 128 / 256 = 16 halfs = 8 half2 per tile - Very manageable. For TILE_K=8: - Each thread loads 8 half2 per tile. But many more tiles (up to 128 for late blocks). - Sync overhead may be non-negligible. TILE_K=16 seems like a good balance. Let me try that. OK, I'll implement the tiled kernel with TILE_K=16, 8 warps per block, each warp handling one row. Actually, with TILE_K=16 and 8 warps per block, I'll use 256 threads per block and process 8 rows per block. Wait, but there could be a lot of __syncthreads with TILE_K=16: - For block 127 (rows 1016..1023), num_keys = 1024. Tiles = ceil(1024/16) = 64 tiles. - 64 iterations of load-sync-compute-sync. - Plus early blocks have fewer tiles but still overhead. For TILE_K=32: - Block 127: ceil(1024/32) = 32 tiles. - Half the sync overhead. Maybe TILE_K=32 is better overall. Let me stick with TILE_K=32 for now. Actually, let me analyze the total work more carefully: With 128 blocks per head, and tiles loaded per block: TILE_K=32: total loads = 67,584 per head TILE_K=64: total loads = 69,632 per head TILE_K=16: total loads = 66,560 per head The difference between 16 and 32 is only ~1.5%. But TILE_K=32 has half the tile iterations. So TILE_K=32 is probably better. For TILE_K=8: total loads = 66,048. vs 67,584 for 32. Difference is ~2.3%. But 4x more iterations. Not worth it. Let me also consider TILE_K=128: Sum ceil((b+1)/16) = 16 * (1 + 2 + ... + 8) = 16 * 36 = 576. Total loads = 128 * 576 = 73,728. More loads but fewer iterations. Might be good if memory coalescing is better. Actually, for loading data into shared memory, larger tiles are MORE efficient due to better coalescing during loads. And fewer syncs. For TILE_K=128: Block 127 (rows 1016..1023): needs ceil(1024/128) = 8 tiles. Very few syncs! But total loads: 73,728. Difference from optimal (~66,000) is about 11%. But fewer syncs might make up for it. For TILE_K=64: 69,632 loads, 16 tiles max. Reasonable middle ground. I think for simplicity, let me try TILE_K=64 first. The shared memory per block is 32KB, and with 32 blocks per SM max, that would be 1MB of shared memory, but we only have 228KB per SM. So we can have at most 7 blocks per SM if each uses 32KB. This reduces occupancy. Actually, occupancy isn't the main concern if the kernel is memory-bound. But we still want good occupancy. With 32KB per block and 228KB per SM, max blocks per SM is 7. That's fine - we have much more work than concurrent capacity. Wait, actually that's not many concurrent blocks... 924 concurrent blocks, each with 256 threads = 236,544 threads. H100 has ~66 warps per SM (looking up typical configs), but let's think about thread capacity. H100 can handle many more warps. Actually, the actual occupancy depends on whether SMEM is the limiting factor. If we use 32KB SMEM, we get 7 blocks per SM. 7 * 256 = 1792 threads per SM. H100 can handle up to 2048 threads per SM (64 warps * 32 threads). So we're at about 87.5% occupancy. Not bad. TILE_K=32 gives 16KB SMEM. 228 / 16 = 14 blocks per SM. 14 * 256 = 3584 threads > 2048 max. So thread count limits us to 2048 threads / 256 = 8 blocks per SM (8 warps * 32 threads = 256 threads per block, 8 blocks = 2048 threads = 64 warps). Actually 64 warps is max for most GPUs. Hmm wait, let me check H100's max threads per SM. Looking at the specs: 64K registers, 255 max per thread, 32 max blocks per SM. Typical max threads is 2048 (64 warps). If we have 256 threads per block, that's 8 blocks to fill the SM thread limit. So TILE_K=32 gives 16KB, allowing 14 blocks by SMEM but only 8 by threads. That's fine, occupancy is determined by threads (8 blocks). TILE_K=64 gives 32KB, allowing 7 blocks by SMEM but again max 8 by threads. So SMEM is the limit: 7 blocks, occupancy 7/8 = 87.5%. I think 87.5% occupancy is good. Let me go with TILE_K=64 for fewer syncs. OK let me implement this now.

// 8\n int kv_head = head * Hkv / Hq;\n int qpos = seq_tile * WARPS + warp_id;\n \n if (qpos >= N) return;\n \n const half* q_ptr = q + (((int64_t)batch * Hq + head) * N + qpos) * D;\n const half* k_ptr = k + (((int64_t)batch * Hkv + kv_head) * N) * D;\n const half* v_ptr = v + (((int64_t)batch * Hkv + kv_head) * N) * D;\n half* o_ptr = out + (((int64_t)batch * Hq + head) * N + qpos) * D;\n \n constexpr int DIMS_PER_THREAD = D / WARP_SIZE;\n \n // Load query into registers\n half2 q2[DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2];\n const half2* q2_ptr = (const half2*)q_ptr;\n #pragma unroll\n for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2; i++) {\n q2[i] = q2_ptr[lane * (DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2) + i];\n }\n \n float o_acc[DIMS_PER_THREAD];\n #pragma unroll\n for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD; i++) o_acc[i] = 0.0f;\n \n float m = -INFINITY;\n float l = 0.0f;\n \n int num_keys = qpos + 1;\n int num_tiles = (num_keys + TILE_K - 1) / TILE_K;\n \n for (int tile = 0; idx < total_halfs; idx += 256) {\n int key_off = idx / D;\n int dim_off = idx % D;\n int glob_key = k_start + key_off;\n v_smem[idx] = v_ptr[glob_key * D + dim_off];\n }\n __syncthreads();\n \n // Compute for this tile\n for (int key = k_start; key < k_end; key++) {\n const half2* k2_ptr = (const half2*)(k_smem + (key - k_start) * D);\n half2 k2[DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2];\n #pragma unroll\n for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2; i++) {\n k2[i] = k2_ptr[lane * (DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2) + i];\n }\n \n float dot = 0.0f;\n #pragma unroll\n for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2; i++) {\n float2 qf = __half22float2(q2[i]);\n float2 kf = __half22float2(k2[i]);\n dot += qf.x * kf.x + qf.y * kf.y;\n }\n dot = warp_reduce_sum<D>(dot) * scale;\n \n float m_prev = m;\n m = fmaxf(m, dot);\n float exp_old = expf(m_prev - m);\n float exp_new = expf(dot - m);\n l = l * exp_old + exp_new;\n \n #pragma unroll\n for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD; i++) {\n o_acc[i] *= exp_old;\n }\n \n const half2* v2_ptr = (const half2*)(v_smem + (key - k_start) * D);\n #pragma unroll\n for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2; i++) {\n half2 v2 = v2_ptr[lane * (DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2) + i];\n float2 vf = __half22float2(v2);\n o_acc[i * 2] += exp_new * vf.x;\n o_acc[i * 2 + 1] += exp_new * vf.y;\n }\n }\n __syncthreads();\n }\n \n float inv_l = 1.0f / l;\n #pragma unroll\n for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD; i++) {\n o_acc[i] *= inv_l;\n }\n \n half2* o2_ptr = (half2*)o_ptr;\n #pragma unroll\n for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2; i++) {\n half2 val;\n val.x = __float2half_rn(o_acc[i * 2]);\n val.y = __float2half_rn(o_acc[i * 2 + 1]);\n o2_ptr[lane * (DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2) + i] = val;\n }\n}\n\ntorch::Tensor attention_forward(torch::Tensor q, torch::Tensor k, torch::Tensor v) {\n int B = q.size(0);\n int Hq = q.size(1);\n int N = q.size(2);\n int D = q.size(3);\n int Hkv = k.size(1);\n \n auto out = torch::empty_like(q);\n \n dim3 block(256);\n constexpr int ROWS_PER_BLOCK = 8;\n dim3 grid((N + ROWS_PER_BLOCK - 1) / ROWS_PER_BLOCK, Hq, B);\n \n float scale = 1.0f / sqrtf((float)D);\n \n switch (D) {\n case 128: {\n constexpr int TILE_K = 64;\n int smem_size = 2 * TILE_K * D * sizeof(half);\n gqa_attention_tiled_kernel<128, TILE_K><<<grid, block, smem_size>>>(\n reinterpret_cast<const half*>(q.data_ptr<at::Half>()),\n reinterpret_cast<const half*>(k.data_ptr<at::Half>()),\n reinterpret_cast<const half*>(v.data_ptr<at::Half>()),\n reinterpret_cast<half*>(out.data_ptr<at::Half>()),\n B, Hq, Hkv, N, scale\n );\n break;\n }\n default:\n TORCH_CHECK(false, \"Unsupported head size: \", D);\n }\n \n return out;\n}\n'''\n\ncpp_source = r'''\ntorch::Tensor attention_forward(torch::Tensor q, torch::Tensor k, torch::Tensor v);\n'''\n\nclass ModelNew(nn.Module):\n def __init__(self, num_heads, num_kv_heads, head_size):\n super().__init__()\n self.num_heads = num_heads\n self.num_kv_heads = num_kv_heads\n self.head_size = head_size\n self._attention = load_inline(\n name=\"gqa_attention_h100_tiled\",\n cpp_sources=cpp_source,\n cuda_sources=CUDA_SOURCE,\n functions=[\"attention_forward\"],\n extra_cuda_cflags=[\"-O3\", \"--use_fast_math\", \"-gencode=arch=compute_90,code=sm_90\"],\n verbose=False,\n )\n\n def forward(self, q, k, v):\n return self._attention.attention_forward(q, k, v)\n" }

The H100 has: - 228KB shared mem per SM - 255 registers per thread max - 3.35 TB/s memory bandwidth - Much higher FP16 tensor core throughput For a standard causal attention with 4 batches x 32 heads x 1024 seq x 128 dim, at batch=4, Hq=32, Hkv=8, N=1024, D=128: Let me think about memory bandwidth. Each element is 2 bytes (fp16). For causal attention: Q reads: batch * Hq * N * D = 4 * 32 * 1024 * 128 = 16,777,216 elements = 32 MB K reads: batch * Hkv * N * D = 4 * 8 * 1024 * 128 = 4,194,304 elements = 8 MB V reads: batch * Hkv * N * D = 4 * 8 * 1024 * 128 = 4,194,304 elements = 8 MB O writes: batch * Hq * N * D = 32 MB Total memory traffic: ~80 MB (but actually K and V are read multiple times due to causal nature - each position reads all previous keys and values) For causal attention, position i reads (i+1) keys. Total K reads = sum_{i=0}^{N-1} (i+1) * Hq/Hkv per sequence. Actually, since all query heads share the same KV heads in GQA, each KV head is read by all associated query heads. For Hq=32, Hkv=8, each KV head serves 4 query heads. Per batch, per KV head, total K reads = sum_{i=0}^{N-1} (i+1) * (number of query heads sharing this KV head) = sum_{i=0}^{N-1} (i+1) * 4 = 4 * N*(N+1)/2 = 2 * N * (N+1) = 2 * 1024 * 1025 ≈ 2M reads per KV head For 8 KV heads and 4 batches: 8 * 4 * 2M ≈ 64M K reads V reads are the same: 64M Total memory reads: Q (4M) + K (64M) + V (64M) = 132M elements = 264 MB Memory bandwidth is 3.35 TB/s = 3350 GB/s So memory-bound time = 264MB / 3350 GB/s ≈ 78.8 microseconds This is definitely memory bandwidth bound, so our optimizations should focus on reducing redundant memory accesses. key <= qpos; key++) {\n const half2* k2_ptr = (const half2*)(k_ptr + key * D);\n half2 k2[DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2];\n #pragma unroll\n for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2; i++) {\n k2[i] = k2_ptr[lane * (DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2) + i];\n }\n \n float dot = 0.0f;\n #pragma unroll\n for (int i = 0; import torch
import torch.nn as nn
from torch.utils.cpp_extension import load_inline

CUDA_SOURCE = r'''
#include <torch/extension.h>
#include <cuda_runtime.h>
#include <cuda_fp16.h>
#include <float.h>

#define WARP_SIZE 32
#define ROWS_PER_BLOCK 8

template <int D>
__device__ __forceinline__ float warp_reduce_sum(float val) {
    #pragma unroll
    for (int offset = 16; offset > 0; offset >>= 1) {
        val += __shfl_xor_sync(0xffffffff, val, offset);
    }
    return val;
}

template <int D>
__launch_bounds__(256, 2)
__global__ void gqa_attention_kernel(
    const half* __restrict__ q,
    const half* __restrict__ k,
    const half* __restrict__ v,
    half* __restrict__ out,
    int B, int Hq, int Hkv, int N, float scale
) {
    int tid = threadIdx.x;
    int warp_id = tid / WARP_SIZE;
    int lane = tid % WARP_SIZE;
    
    int head = blockIdx.y;
    int batch = blockIdx.z;
    int seq_tile = blockIdx.x;
    
    int kv_head = head * Hkv / Hq;
    int qpos = seq_tile * ROWS_PER_BLOCK + warp_id;
    
    if (qpos >= N) return;
    
    const half* q_ptr = q + (((int64_t)batch * Hq + head) * N + qpos) * D;
    const half* k_ptr = k + (((int64_t)batch * Hkv + kv_head) * N) * D;
    const half* v_ptr = v + (((int64_t)batch * Hkv + kv_head) * N) * D;
    half* o_ptr = out + (((int64_t)batch * Hq + head) * N + qpos) * D;
    
    constexpr int DIMS_PER_THREAD = D / WARP_SIZE;
    
    // Load query - vectorized half2
    half2 q2[DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2];
    const half2* q2_ptr = (const half2*)q_ptr;
    #pragma unroll
    for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2; i++) {
        q2[i] = q2_ptr[lane * (DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2) + i];
    }
    
    float o_acc[DIMS_PER_THREAD];
    #pragma unroll
    for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD; i++) o_acc[i] = 0.0f;
    
    float m = -INFINITY;
    float l = 0.0f;
    
    // Main loop over keys
    for (int key = 0; key <= qpos; key++) {
        const half2* k2_ptr = (const half2*)(k_ptr + key * D);
        half2 k2[DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2];
        #pragma unroll
        for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2; i++) {
            k2[i] = k2_ptr[lane * (DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2) + i];
        }
        
        float dot = 0.0f;
        #pragma unroll
        for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2; i++) {
            float2 qf = __half22float2(q2[i]);
            float2 kf = __half22float2(k2[i]);
            dot += qf.x * kf.x + qf.y * kf.y;
        }
        dot = warp_reduce_sum<D>(dot) * scale;
        
        float m_prev = m;
        m = fmaxf(m, dot);
        float exp_old = expf(m_prev - m);
        float exp_new = expf(dot - m);
        l = l * exp_old + exp_new;
        
        #pragma unroll
        for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD; i++) {
            o_acc[i] *= exp_old;
        }
        
        const half2* v2_ptr = (const half2*)(v_ptr + key * D);
        #pragma unroll
        for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2; i++) {
            half2 v2 = v2_ptr[lane * (DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2) + i];
            float2 vf = __half22float2(v2);
            o_acc[i * 2] += exp_new * vf.x;
            o_acc[i * 2 + 1] += exp_new * vf.y;
        }
    }
    
    float inv_l = 1.0f / l;
    #pragma unroll
    for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD; i++) {
        o_acc[i] *= inv_l;
    }
    
    // Store - vectorized
    half2* o2_ptr = (half2*)o_ptr;
    #pragma unroll
    for (int i = 0; i < DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2; i++) {
        half2 val;
        val.x = __float2half_rn(o_acc[i * 2]);
        val.y = __float2half_rn(o_acc[i * 2 + 1]);
        o2_ptr[lane * (DIMS_PER_THREAD / 2) + i] = val;
    }
}

torch::Tensor attention_forward(torch::Tensor q, torch::Tensor k, torch::Tensor v) {
    int B = q.size(0);
    int Hq = q.size(1);
    int N = q.size(2);
    int D = q.size(3);
    int Hkv = k.size(1);
    
    auto out = torch::empty_like(q);
    
    dim3 block(256);
    dim3 grid((N + ROWS_PER_BLOCK - 1) / ROWS_PER_BLOCK, Hq, B);
    
    float scale = 1.0f / sqrtf((float)D);
    
    switch (D) {
        case 128:
            gqa_attention_kernel<128><<<grid, block>>>(
                reinterpret_cast<const half*>(q.data_ptr<at::Half>()),
                reinterpret_cast<const half*>(k.data_ptr<at::Half>()),
                reinterpret_cast<const half*>(v.data_ptr<at::Half>()),
                reinterpret_cast<half*>(out.data_ptr<at::Half>()),
                B, Hq, Hkv, N, scale
            );
            break;
        default:
            TORCH_CHECK(false, "Unsupported head size: ", D);
    }
    
    return out;
}
'''

cpp_source = r'''
torch::Tensor attention_forward(torch::Tensor q, torch::Tensor k, torch::Tensor v);
'''

class ModelNew(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, num_heads, num_kv_heads, head_size):
        super().__init__()
        self.num_heads = num_heads
        self.num_kv_heads = num_kv_heads
        self.head_size = head_size
        self._attention = load_inline(
            name="gqa_attention_h100_opt2",
            cpp_sources=cpp_source,
            cuda_sources=CUDA_SOURCE,
            functions=["attention_forward"],
            extra_cuda_cflags=["-O3", "--use_fast_math", "-gencode=arch=compute_90,code=sm_90"],
            verbose=False,
        )

    def forward(self, q, k, v):
        return self._attention.attention_forward(q, k, v)
